#7144. Alvernia University to acquire Marian University

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The two Catholic institutions, which are hundreds of miles apart, said the merger would be based on shared values rather than financial necessity.

Alvernia said neither university is entering the merger due to financial trouble.

“Both institutions are approaching this opportunity from a position of strength, guided by a shared commitment to mission alignment, long-term stewardship and expanded opportunities for students,” Alvernia said in its FAQ.

But when compared on financial strength, the two universities are in different weight classes.

Alvernia has posted multimillion budget surpluses over the past decade, outside of a reduced surplus in fiscal 2020 and a small deficit in fiscal 2024. In contrast, Marian’s finances have fluctuated more, and it has run a deficit since fiscal 2023.

The difference likely comes down to enrollment, as it does at most tuition-dependent colleges.

Alvernia had 2,796 students in fall 2024, a 2.4% increase from five years prior, according to federal data. Marian’s student body, meanwhile, declined 24.8% to 1,246 over the same period.

In 2023, Marian leadership declared an enrollment emergency and enacted a round of cuts, eliminating over 20 academic programs and laying off an undisclosed number of staff.

Marian Board Chair Holly Brenner said Monday that the merger with Alvernia will allow Marian to preserve its legacy and create new opportunities.

“Throughout this process, protecting Marian’s mission and identity has been at the heart of our discussions,” Brenner said in a statement. “We believe this proposed path forward with Alvernia offers an opportunity to build on Marian’s strong foundation, create new possibilities for our students, and carry our mission forward into the future.”

In turn, Michael Duff, chair of Alvernia’s board, called the acquisition “a thoughtful, mission-aligned step” for the university, one that “reflects Alvernia’s strength, its disciplined stewardship and its commitment to pursuing opportunities that are consistent with our mission.”

The proposed merger is pending regulatory and accreditor approval, as well as formal sign-off from the universities’ boards and affiliated religious orders. Both institutions said they will seek input from faculty and campus groups as details are hammered out.

#7139. Penn State wrestles with $124M budget hole

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The public institution projects a $87 million year-over-year drop in tuition revenue amid a fall enrollment decline.

Just over 70% of Penn State’s budget hole is due to falling tuition and fee revenue.

Behind the revenue miss is a fall headcount that is 3,058 students short of the comparable 2025 number. Breaking that down, Penn State said Monday that it enrolled 742 fewer graduate students, 922 fewer international students and 2,384 fewer out-of-state students across its campuses. All three of those groups can have an outsized impact on tuition revenue at a college.

About half of the enrollment decline came at seven regional campuses that are set to close — and had been expected. In explaining the declines, the university also pointed to nationwide trends, including widespread decreases in graduate enrollment and international students.

At the same time, Penn State logged its largest-ever undergraduate class of 43,010 students and a record class of 9,907 first-time, full-time students.

“Even with these challenges, Penn State's market position is strong,” said Matt Melvin, Penn State vice president for enrollment management, in a statement.

Health insurance costs made up the remainder of the university’s fiscal 2027 shortfall in its $2.2 billion education and general budget. By August, healthcare claims had grown 15.7% year over year, the university said Monday. Budgetwise, that led to a $37 million jump above projections.

On an FAQ page about its budget, Penn State said it had no plans for broad cuts to its programs or student services, or for changes in its hiring processes, as it looks to tighten spending. The institution also said it would hold tuition and fee prices steady despite the revenue dip.

The university does, however, already have plans to nix some 47 undergraduate programs after a multiyear review of its offerings. Collectively, those programs enroll about 1.1% of Penn State’s undergraduates. The institution listed another 50 programs as “strategic growth opportunities.”

Penn State’s plan to close seven of its 19 regional Commonwealth campuses is primarily an effort to shore up the institution’s budget. Keeping those locations open would cost the university $40 million annually in financial support and overhead expenses and require a $200 million investment in facilities, according to a 2025 report recommending the closures.

#7138. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

#7137. Senior Deliverability and Compliance Specialist at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Most Kit creators don't have a dedicated Creator Growth Manager, and when their emails start landing in spam, they need someone who can find the cause quickly and explain it in plain language. We're looking for a Deliverability and Compliance Specialist to own deliverability support for that long tail of creators, run Kit's abuse desk, and make the compliance calls that protect the sending reputation every creator on Kit shares.

You'll also build the playbooks, training, and AI-assisted workflows that help Support and contractors handle more deliverability questions on their own, and you'll partner with our infrastructure engineers as we run more of our sending in-house. A creator can't build a lasting business if their emails don't arrive, so this work connects directly to our mission.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes digging through headers and bounce logs to find the real cause of a problem, can explain DMARC alignment to a first-time creator without jargon, and is comfortable making a firm call when a situation is gray. You don't wait for someone to tell you what needs fixing. You proactively spot opportunities, identify patterns, and take the initiative to improve deliverability before problems arise.

You'll report to Alyssa Dulin, Director of Creator Success, and collaborate closely with:

Our Deliverability team: a Deliverability Lead who supports our highest-value accounts and leads deliverability education, and a Deliverability Specialist who onboards and ramps up large senders moving to Kit

Our infrastructure engineers, who run Kit's sending infrastructure

Our compliance and anti-abuse engineers, who build the automated checks that keep spammers off Kit

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

Customer Success Managers (we call them Creator Growth Managers at Kit), Sales and Migrations, and Product Managers

First Week: Get set up in Kit, our admin tools, and our support and ticketing systems, and meet our distributed team in Get To Know You chats (GTKYs). Meet the deliverability team and get an introduction on the current projects. Read our deliverability playbooks and SOPs, and get access to the monitoring tools you'll use every day: Google Postmaster Tools, Microsoft SNDS, blocklist monitoring, and our internal delivery dashboards.

First Month: Shadow the Deliverability team and Product Specialists on deliverability tickets, account approvals, and abuse reports, then start answering queue tickets with review. Join the daily monitoring rotation (blocklist delisting, Postmaster and delivery monitoring) and the weekly Deliverability team meeting. Learn how account approvals, compliance flags, and our in-house sending platform work. By the end of the month, work deliverability tickets and abuse reports on your own and send your first delisting requests. Project: build a recurring report showing the trending deliverability ticket reasons so we understand what’s causing how to reduce friction for customers and how to solve it

First Six Months: Own the deliverability support queue for creators without a dedicated CGM: spam-folder placement, bounces, authentication and DMARC setup, and escalations from Product Specialists. Own compliance end to end, from new-account approvals and compliance flags to pausing or closing accounts that put our shared reputation at risk.Run Kit's abuse desk. You'll write and share a strategy that covers coverage, response-time targets, escalation paths, and quality checks, then put it in place so routine spam, phishing, and blocklist reports follow clear SOPs while you take the hardest cases yourself. Be the go-to contact for day-to-day mailbox provider issues with Microsoft, Gmail, Yahoo, Comcast, and regional providers, and write an incident runbook for blocklistings and provider throttling. Track the queue with a simple scorecard (volume, first response time, and escalation rate) and share what you learn with the team. Run training sessions for Support and ship your first process or AI improvements, like cleaner ticket categorization or better account data for our AI support agent, so more deliverability tickets get solved without escalation..

First Year: Deliverability support is faster and more consistent for every creator on Kit, including the ones who never talk to a CGM. Your playbooks, help center articles, and AI-assisted workflows are what Support, CGMs, and contractors reach for first, and fewer tickets need to come to you. You've led at least one cross-team fix with infrastructure for a recurring problem you spotted in the queue, such as an alert when a creator's domain authentication breaks or a check for sudden signup spikes. You're helping shape the internal tooling and alerting our in-house sending needs, and you take part in industry groups like M3AAWG.

Deep working knowledge of email authentication (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, alignment, and BIMI basics), plus the ability to read raw message headers and SMTP bounce and deferral codes

Hands-on use of Google Postmaster Tools and Microsoft SNDS/JMRP, and comfort working with major blocklists (Spamhaus, Abusix, Barracuda, Spamcop, and others) and their delisting processes

Data fluency: you can pull bounce, complaint, and engagement data together, find the root cause, and explain it so others can act on it

Hands-on abuse handling: you can work an abuse report or escalation start to finish, from finding the account and assessing the evidence to acting, replying to the reporter, and documenting the decision

Process building: you can turn repeat work into SOPs, playbooks, and quality checks that a contractor or teammate can follow

Clear, calm written communication with creators, teammates, and postmasters

Using AI tools to research, summarize, and draft, and helping teammates do the same, with the judgment to keep a person on final compliance calls

Experience with MTA logs or delivery observability tools (KumoMTA, PowerMTA, Postfix, Kibana/Elasticsearch, or SQL and BI tools like Mode)

Familiarity with feedback loops, spam traps, list bombing, and bot-signup patterns

Experience with Intercom, HubSpot, or a similar ticketing tool

Experience building or tuning AI support workflows or macros

4+ years in email deliverability, compliance, trust and safety, or technical support at an ESP, mailbox provider, or high-volume sender

Handling escalated customer tickets end to end, including with customers who are frustrated

Making account-level compliance or abuse decisions, such as approving, suspending, or closing accounts

Working an abuse desk or abuse mailbox directly: spam and phishing complaints, provider and blocklist reports, and escalations

Leading the fix for a recurring deliverability or compliance problem that needed help from more than one team

Supporting senders on shared IP pools at an ESP

Setting abuse or trust and safety strategy, or directing contractors or vendors on a support or abuse queue

Working directly with mailbox provider postmaster teams

Participating in M3AAWG or other industry groups

Creating educational content, like help docs, training, workshops, or podcasts

Working with creators, digital entrepreneurs, or small businesses

You pick up problems that fall between teams without waiting for permission, and you follow them through until they're closed.

You test a hypothesis against the evidence before you act, and you frame problems clearly enough that others trust your analysis.

#7135. Baylor stares down $30M deficit after undergraduate enrollment dip

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Baptist institution in Texas is pausing hiring and delaying capital spending as it tries to shore up the budget gap.

Livingstone’s deficit announcement follows a slide in Baylor’s undergraduate enrollment this fall.

Baylor’s total undergraduate headcount fell 2.3% year over year to 13,860 students, according to institutional data. First-time, first-year enrollment dipped 6%, representing a decline of over 200 students, which could bring ongoing financial ramifications given that they might have continued paying tuition for several years.

“Undergraduate enrollment is realigning to historic levels now that the two large COVID-era freshman classes have matriculated through the University,” Livingstone said. The university brought record numbers of students in 2020 and 2021, bucking the national trend during that period.

However, graduate enrollment increased by 3.8% to 5,888 students.

Livingstone rattled off a long list of headwinds that have dogged Baylor’s budget. Among them were rising spending, families wary of college prices, competitors increasingly discounting tuition to recruit students and “increasing volatility in forecasting budgets in an uncertain financial environment.”

Baylor’s leader also acknowledged the sum the university owes the head football coach it just fired after a slow start to the season. The buyout reportedly amounts to an estimated $12 million for Dave Aranda. Livingstone described the costs as a one-time expense not included in the deficit and “a liability of Baylor Athletics that will be addressed solely by the department.”

In the previous fiscal year, the Division I university brought in about $63 million in revenue from college sports, according to its financial statements.

Baylor started the calendar year in cost-cutting mode. In February, Livingstone announced a plan to shave $35 million from the university’s budget over two years, which she framed as necessary to limit tuition increases on students. Among other moves, the university slashed retirement contributions for employees.

In her message Monday, the university president acknowledged the sacrifices Baylor’s workforce has already made to help shore up the budget.

“As faithful Christian stewards, we must work together to prepare Baylor to succeed in a very uncertain future for colleges and universities that is likely to look quite different than the past,” she said.

Baylor joins a handful of others to issue early reports on fiscal 2027 woes amid enrollment pressures.

Just this week, Boston University leaders said the private institution missed revenue estimates by $42 million so far, and Pennsylvania State University revealed a projected $124 million budget hole due primarily to tuition revenue declines. Both institutions plan on budget cuts but did not signal that layoffs would be part of the savings efforts.

#7133. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

#7131. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

AI security is entering a new stage as enterprise organizations look beyond protecting models from prompt injection, unauthorized access, and data exposure.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7130. Colleges report declines in international enrollment amid federal restrictions

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Survey data released Wednesday found that nearly 6 in 10 U.S. colleges lost foreign graduate students this fall, and 45% saw a decline in undergraduates.

Wednesday’s survey, released by NAFSA: Association of International Educators in partnership with the Oxford University Press and the student recruitment platform Studyportals, reinforces concerns that the Trump administration's attacks on foreign students and the long-standing U.S. programs that support their study here would corrode the international enrollment pipeline.

Survey respondents cited the administration’s crackdown on student visas and the astronomical new fees sought for the H-1B visa and Optional Practical Training programs. While some of those policies are being challenged in the courts, they can signal to international students that they are unwelcome in the U.S., and the uncertainty and instability can ward off prospective applicants.

Major public research universities, traditionally popular among foreign students, have begun reporting international enrollment declines this fall. And some, like Pennsylvania State University, have drawn a direct line between that loss of students and budgetary challenges.

“These survey results affirm what we’ve observed before: national policies matter to international students making major decisions about where to study, conduct research, or start a career,” Fanta Aw, NAFSA’s executive director and CEO, said in a statement Wednesday.

Some colleges also reported decreased interest in countries that have historically sent the most higher education students overseas, such as China and India.

“Our agents in India in particular are telling us that they are seeing a drastic decline in inquiries for US graduate study and that many prospective students are either considering other destinations or postponing study plans,” one university respondent said.

Of those surveyed, 37% of U.S. colleges said they planned to diversify the locations where they recruit from and tap into new markets over the next 12 months.

One institution said it would pivot to the Philippines, Canada and Colombia as a means of offsetting losses from India, Bangladesh and Nepal, the three countries from which the college traditionally got the most foreign students.

Another 32% said they would expand their online offerings, and 30% said they would turn to budget cuts.

However, cuts could further exacerbate challenges for colleges’ international admissions offices. One respondent admitted that staffing issues have “resulted in a lack of attention to incoming students.”

Pollsters surveyed colleges’ senior administrators and international student recruitment professionals online between Oct. 5 and 26.

#7124. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

#7121. Which states plan to opt into the federal school choice program?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

States have until Jan. 1, 2027, to tell the IRS they will join the Education Freedom Tax Credit program.

More than half the states have indicated to the IRS that they will participate in the new federal Education Freedom Tax Credit program that begins Jan. 1 and is projected to direct billions of dollars in taxpayer donations to private and public school services through student scholarships.

Even though rules for the program are not yet finalized by the U.S. Department of the Treasury and the IRS, states and the scholarship-granting organizations that will manage the scholarships can rely on proposed and temporary guidance as they prepare for the program's launch.

Only states that opt into the Republican-led program can participate, and in addition to the 30 states that have formally told the IRS they are interested, at least two other states — New York and New Mexico — have publicly announced they will also take part in the first national school choice program.

The midterm elections saw five state governorships — Georgia, Iowa, Nevada, Ohio and Texas — flip from Republican to Democrat and some state legislative chambers also gain Democratic majorities. This blue wave could affect which states ultimately participate in the federal school choice program.

States must inform the IRS by Jan. 1 of their election into the program and finalize their decision to participate by Feb. 15 with their submission of approved scholarship-granting organizations. Those SGOs will manage and distribute student scholarships.

#7118. What principals can do to ease new teachers’ anxieties

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Implementing open-door policies and placing veteran educators in mentor roles are among key strategies, school leaders say.

How can principals and other school leaders give teachers a safe way to express worries or anxieties, and how does that differ for newer and more experienced teachers?

For Joe Patek, board member of the National Principals Association and principal at Nicolet Union High School in Glendale, Wisconsin, the process starts with providing “authentic spaces” to have an “honest dialogue,” recognizing that this will be awkward at first until you build trust. “You’re trying to build bridges so staff know, ‘We’ll be in the weeds with you.’”

Evelyn Edney, principal of Early College School at Delaware State University and another NPA board member, also centers relationship-building.

“People need to trust you to share [anxieties] with you,” she said, adding that it’s important for them to feel confident that you’re the person they can sit down and talk to. “When you say you have an open-door policy, you’ve got to mean that. Shut the door and [say], ‘If you need to yell, scream and cuss, let’s do it. Then let’s get to the root of the problem.’”

Building trust and promoting an open-door policy provides reassurance that you will truly listen and be solutions-minded rather than simply saying “thanks” and never meaningfully addressing the worries expressed, said Ann-Marie Gleason, president of the National Association of Elementary School Principals and principal of Harpeth Valley Elementary in Nashville, Tennessee.

It means “when folks come to you, they know that you are going to listen to them,” she said.

Once trust is established, principals can prompt teachers to openly share their stressors, both generally and regarding a specific situation or student, and then help address those issues, Patek said. Showing up in classrooms is one key to reaching that point.

“You’d be surprised how many conversations happen at the end of a five-minute [classroom] activity,” he said. “It opens the door to those authentic conversations and brings down barriers between classrooms and the principal’s office.”

Edney said she tries to alleviate stress whenever and however she can.

“I’ve had days where we have somebody practically breaking down,” she said. “I say, ‘You know what? Take the day and go home.’ They’re not going to be able to do school. Sometimes, kids do change your mood. But it’s hard when you have something gnawing at your soul.”

When concerns impact more than one teacher, Gleason suggests having a leadership team in place within grade levels and within academic departments to address those on a more comprehensive basis. That’s partly to provide different options for outlets.

“If a teacher didn’t feel comfortable initially coming straight to an administrator, they would also have that grade-level liaison to express that concern,” she said.

Newer teachers need resources to gain knowledge of local context, including from mentors while they’re onboarding and — if possible — staff who are also parents and can give a sense of how, for example, a certain politically divisive issue plays in the community, Patek said.

“What are the land mines?” he said. More experienced teachers tend to need reassurance that their skills and knowledge remain applicable as times change, he added. “While they’re seeing decades of change happening, it really is the same profession with the same mission.”

Edney said that new teacher orientation at Early College School gives way to monthly check-ins to make sure “newbies” are navigating the role well, which “takes away the angst of being in a new system,” she said.

Veteran teachers are tapped to be mentors, which helps them “feel valued and useful in school, probably even more so because of their experience,” said Edney. “They’ve been there. They’ve seen that. They’ve done that.”

At Harpeth Valley, Gleason said, new teachers and paraprofessionals are paired with longer-term staff, and specialized conversations are designed based on particular contexts and needs.

“Some best practices are the same. Some are unique to the building you’re working in,” she said. “For veteran teachers … when we make a change in curriculum or delivery or instruction or processes, it’s always important for school administrators to explain the ‘why’ behind the change. It can cause anxiety if they don’t understand the reason.”

#7116. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7114. Majority of students report misusing tech during school hours

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A Fordham Institute report suggests stricter bans are needed for cellphones and other personal devices, as many students still use technology to cheat.

The findings from Fordham come as the nation's largest school districts this fall announced restrictions around AI use.

In September, Los Angeles Unified School District, the nation's largest school district, announced it would restrict access to AI platforms for all students during the 2026-27 school year. The restrictions will be in place as the district reviews instructional use and safeguards for the technology, according to the district's announcement.

New York City Public Schools also announced in September it would pause AI use for elementary and middle school students.

“Though we might quibble about the details, we at Fordham agree with the impulse,” wrote Brian Fitzpatrick, national research manager at Fordham and the report’s author.

"Taken together, our results suggest that American high school students are caught up in — and often contribute to — a disturbing and interconnected set of problems: rampant distraction, widespread AI misuse, heavy dependence on screens, and grading practices that make it easier to earn high marks without mastering the material," said Fitzpatrick in the analysis.

The report was based on a nationally representative survey of over 2,000 current students and recent graduates, fielded between February and March 2026.

It found that over 50% of students use technology during the school day for social media, and just under that percentage use it to message friends. A third of students said they used it for both recreational games and YouTube viewing unrelated to assignments.

"A key reason that illicit tech use is so pervasive is that school-issued devices provide direct access to the platforms driving it," the report said. For example, two-thirds of respondents said YouTube is accessible on school-issued devices.

The report also found students were least likely to use social media when phones were fully banned, such as keeping them in a locked pouch or at the central office. Students were more likely to use social media if they were allowed access to their phones in hallways and at lunch time or if their phones were only restricted during class times.

That finding echoes another report published by the Brookings Institution in September, which found that cellphone ban enforcement tends to wane over the course of the school year as teacher enforcement wanes overtime. Bell-to-bell bans, the report found, may be more effective and durable overtime.

However, some have pushed back against all-day cellphone bans due to safety concerns for students, who cite school shootings and other emergencies as reasons to keep phones closer.

#7111. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

AI security is entering a new stage as enterprise organizations look beyond protecting models from prompt injection, unauthorized access, and data exposure.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7110. Broward schools ‘no longer the only game in town’ as enrollment drops

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Florida district is doubling down on student recruitment and retention by stepping up marketing and engagement efforts.

This is the second installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

When Valerie Wanza started as a teacher in Florida's Broward County Public Schools in 1992, the district was at the beginning of what would be explosive growth in student population over the next decade.

Throughout the 1990s and early 2000s, Broward County Public Schools saw such an increase in enrollment that by 2004, more than 100 of its 315 schools were deemed "critically overcrowded."

Now, 34 years later as the district's chief strategy and innovation officer, Wanza is part of a team of administrators working to turn around an enrollment decline that began in the 2015-16 school year. The student population plummeted by 17.5% over the next decade and is forecast to continue decreasing through at least the 2030-31 school year, according to district data.

The district attributed the drop to various factors: lower birth rates leading to fewer children entering schools, rising housing costs that make it harder for families to live in the desirable area bordering the Atlantic Ocean, fewer immigrant students due to shifts in federal policies, and growing competition from private school choice options.

And with the area's school choice proliferation, Wanza said, Broward County Public Schools is "no longer the only game in town."

Now, to stay financially healthy, the district is taking a comprehensive approach that includes consolidating and closing some school campuses, cutting at least 1,000 staff positions, and implementing other measures to save money. It's also attempting to boost enrollment by vigorously recruiting new students through media campaigns, personalized phone calls, and hosting events to drum up interest in and excitement for the district's schools.

Broward is even turning to an outside vendor for help. Earlier this year, the district approved a one-year contract with student recruitment firm Caissa K12 to help connect with families who have left its public schools, determine their reasons for doing so, attempt to resolve those concerns and inform them about the district's benefits and schooling options.

The performance-based contract said Broward County Public Schools will pay Caissa K12 $850 per recruited student who enrolls in a district school, up to 176 students or a maximum payment of $150,000.

“If we aren’t real about, No. 1, recognizing what adjustments we need to make, and then be bold enough to make them, we’re going to become the Kmart … of the public schools,” Wanza said, referring to the once-dominant discount retailer that filed for bankruptcy in 2002 amid growing competition and other pressures.

The enrollment decline that Broward County Public Schools is experiencing mirrors the challenges faced by many other school districts across the country.

While falling birth rates and rising school choice are often to blame, the multitude of factors can be complex and specific to each region's ability to draw new residents with young families. Nationally, enrollment in traditional public schools is expected to drop from 54.7 million in 2021 to 49.4 million in 2035, according to a 2025 analysis from the Brookings Institution based on U.S. Census and other federal data.

On the other hand, some states are predicted to see increases in public school student populations between fall 2022 and fall 2031. Among those are Idaho (+11%), North Dakota (+7.4%) and Tennessee (+2%), according to data from the U.S. Department of Education.

Enrollment in Florida's public schools is projected to increase by 3% over the same time period.

From 2015-16 through 2025-26, Broward County Public Schools lost 17.5% of its student population, or 38,640 students. That translated to a loss of $352 million in per-pupil revenue over that 11-year span. In fiscal year 2026, which ended June 30, the district expected to show an $80 million shortfall because of continued enrollment loss.

The district has already closed seven schools since the 2025-26 school year and is exploring other closures, consolidations and grade configurations for the future. New data released this fall shows district enrollment dropped by another 10,272 for the 2026-27 school year.

Even so, with 315 schools educating 224,324 students, including at charter schools, Broward County Public Schools is still the 6th largest district in the U.S., according to its website.

Broward leaders are carefully analyzing data that can explain why families aren't enrolling their students in county schools. Joseph Beck, the district's director of demographics and enrollment planning, said his team is constantly coming up with ways to look at the data differently to better understand why students are coming or going.

For instance, the data shows that about 20,000 students, on average, withdraw from Broward County Public Schools annually. That number, which doesn't include graduating seniors, has been consistent over many years, Beck said.

The district also investigates how many new students enroll each year, a figure that can be affected by immigration policy changes. For instance, the district had 3,400 fewer newcomer students in 2025-26 than it did the previous year, Beck said.

The data also shows that 1,700 students enrolled in the 2024-25 school year transferred to a private school the following year using a Florida Family Empowerment Scholarship to help pay tuition.

At the state level, about 561,000 students participated in taxpayer-funded scholarship programs during the 2025-26 school year, according to a Florida Senate analysis. That same year, the state's public schools enrolled about 2.8 million students, state data shows.

Across Broward County, some 35,000 students participated in one of Florida's taxpayer-supported private school choice programs in the 2025-26 school year.

Beck said the data further shows that many of the county's private school choice participants weren't previously enrolled in the district's public schools. "They just completely bypass us altogether," Beck said.

He and other Broward County Public School leaders are aiming to reverse that.

Farrah Wilson, Broward County Public Schools' director of marketing and strategic communications, rapidly ticks off the efforts being made to court new students and their families. There are mailers, social media campaigns, TV ads, school tours, home visits and more.

As public schools across the country get more intentional and strategic about recruitment, they are taking a playbook from private schools. That includes hosting school tours, having "shadow days" for prospective students, and sending mailers to families encouraging them to add public schools to their list of options.

We have the best programs. We have the best teachers. We have the best facilities, athletics. We offer a comprehensive experience that many of our charter and private schools can't offer.

Broward County Public Schools' director of marketing and strategic communications

Broward County is home to 245 private schools serving about 54,000 students, or about 18% of students in the county, according to Private School Review, a site that provides information on K-12 private schools for families.

But competition for students can also come from nearby public school systems.

In a mailer that Broward sent out in mid-July, it targeted families living across the district's borders to the north in Palm Beach County and to the south in Miami-Dade County.

"This is just a way to let parents know that there are options, and if they are interested, they can go to one of our schools on the county line and participate and take one of the open seats," Wilson said.

The mailers led to one student from Palm Beach County and 17 students from Miami-Dade County enrolling in Broward County Public Schools, the district said.

While it might seem unfair to try to poach other districts' students, Broward administrators said it goes both ways. The School District of Palm Beach County has likewise attempted to recruit Broward students living near its border, Broward officials said.

The Palm Beach system shares information about its schools through various channels, and marketing reaches families across the region, including those in neighboring counties like Broward, said Sean Cooley, chief of communications and enrollment for the Palm Beach district, in an Oct. 29 email. However, Palm Beach does not track how many Broward residents enroll in its schools.

"Many families living near county boundaries commute to work within Palm Beach County, making our high-performing schools an attractive and convenient option for their children's education," Cooley said.

Florida has an open boundary policy that lets families enroll in schools outside of their home school district as long as there are open seats available. "We're used to having open lines, if you will. Now we just got more intentional about it," said Wanza.

Broward is also expanding its school choice options by catering to what families want. The district has an online school as well as 32 schools offering dual language programs. It has magnet schools, a high school that operates on a 4-day-a-week schedule, several K-8 schools, a grade 6-12 school and a K-12 school.

In another enrollment initiative, the district is encouraging and supporting each school to tell its own stories through various marketing and communications efforts. For example, on "testimonial Tuesdays," students, families and staff share on social media why their school is great.

Initiatives like this enable families to make connections with individual schools, not necessarily the district as a whole, Wilson said. Building excitement at individual schools is "the secret sauce" to boosting enrollments, she said.

Every school also has an "Enroll Now" button on its website. If a family clicks the link and shares their contact information, the school's principal is automatically notified and is encouraged to reach out directly to the family, answer any questions they have, offer a tour and make other recruiting efforts that will "close the deal," Wilson said.

One reason Broward is taking such an intentional approach to marketing, said administrators, is to combat misinformation or outdated information about district schools, including about its academic performance. Broward County Public Schools has earned an "A" rating from the Florida Department of Education for three consecutive years and continues to "demonstrate strong academic growth," the district said in an email to K-12 Dive.

Negative perceptions of the public school system are what the district is "always battling against," Wilson said. This is why the district is heavily investing in marketing and recruiting, she said.

"We have the best programs. We have the best teachers. We have the best facilities, athletics. We offer a comprehensive experience that many of our charter and private schools can't offer.”

News Graphic Developer Jasmine Ye Han contributed to this story.

#7109. Product Manager - Postgres at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're hiring a Product Manager to own Postgres itself at Supabase. Every Supabase project is a full Postgres database, and Postgres is the foundation that Auth, Storage, Realtime and Edge Functions run on. You'll own the product experience of the database: Postgres versions and upgrades, maintenance, observability, extensions and security.

You'll work closely with our Postgres engineering teams and with the PM lead for Databases, who leads Multigres. You'll define the Postgres requirements that architecture must meet, and own how customers experience their database through that transition.

This role is ideal for someone who loves Postgres, sweats the details that make a database trustworthy, and is excited about improving the experience for millions of developers. Small improvements here compound across every project on the platform.

You'll own Postgres versions, upgrades and end-of-life. Which major and minor versions we support, how and when customers upgrade, and how we retire old versions. Your goal is to make upgrades routine rather than risky.

You'll make maintenance predictable. You'll design how we schedule, communicate and run maintenance windows so customers stay informed and in control, and disruption is rare and expected.

You'll help developers understand their database. Query performance, metrics, logs and advisors: you'll own the path from "my database is slow" to the fix.

You'll own our extension ecosystem. Which extensions we offer, how we evaluate and support new ones, and how we keep them working across versions.

You'll own Postgres security. Roles and permissions, Row Level Security ergonomics, secure defaults, and how we respond to Postgres and extension CVEs.

You'll talk to developers across the full spectrum, from indie developers on their first project to enterprises running critical workloads, and bring what you learn into every prioritization call.

You'll own the problem definition, requirements and success metrics for what we ship, such as upgrade success rate, the share of the fleet on supported versions, and time-to-diagnose for performance problems. You'll set the metric before the team builds, then track it after launch.

You'll keep Engineering, Support and GTM aligned. You'll own the plan of record for what's coming, why, and in what order, and communicate version changes and deprecations clearly to customers.

4+ years of Product Management experience on databases, developer tools or infrastructure, or equivalent depth as an engineer, DBA or founder moving into product. You're comfortable commenting on an architecture doc and advocating for the user when trade-offs are required.

Know Postgres well, or another relational database well enough to ramp quickly. You're comfortable reading EXPLAIN output and talking about replication, WAL, roles and extensions.

Have shipped products where reliability and trust are the experience. You treat downtime and breaking changes as product problems, not just operational ones.

Write clearly. Your specs, announcements and deprecation notices leave little room for confusion.

Build with what you own. You run queries, test upgrades and try extensions yourself, and form opinions through first-hand use.

Use AI to move faster. You use AI tools to compress research, drafting and feedback synthesis so you spend more time on judgment calls.

Work async by default. Supabase is fully remote. You make decisions in writing and move work forward without meetings.

Ran Postgres in production: upgrades, tuning, incident response.

Worked on database observability, performance tooling, or security and compliance products.

Contributed to Postgres, its extensions, or the wider open-source community.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.
