#7057. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

AI security is entering a new stage as enterprise organizations look beyond protecting models from prompt injection, unauthorized access, and data exposure.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7052. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

#7047. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#7041. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#7037. We’d Kill to See These MobLand Spin-Offs

With season 2 racing toward its finale and season 3 still some way off, we put our imagination to work.

If ever there were a case for “if something’s worth doing, it’s worth overdoing,” it’s MobLand. I don’t know who first said it—someone impersonating Guy Ritchie, probably.

Of all Ritchie’s gangland offspring, MobLand sits at the very tip—the tip of the iceberg, tip of the tongue, anonymous tip. It has already been tipped for awards this season.

Season 3 is understood to be in production, due sometime in late 2027. But as the second outing hurtles towards its finale this month, the wait looks positively Homeric. So, in the spirit of overdoing, here are a few spin-offs nobody asked for but richly deserve.

Newgate, the early 2000s. Kevin is a broken young man after an assault by a mean prison guard called Rusby (remember him? Kevin kills him in revenge in MobLand season 1), and he is one bad night from hanging himself with a bedsheet. His cellmate Harry, a quiet new arrival with a thousand-yard stare, talks him down. Then he does something tougher and kinder: he decides Kevin is going to live, whether Kevin likes it or not.

What follows is a double act forged in a concrete box. Harry fights, bargains and bluffs his way up the wing’s pecking order, while Kevin learns to be a person again, ultimately becoming his gobby, hapless sidekick. Between the long, dark stretches in their cell come the scams, the contraband and the beatings. A snooker table in the rec room becomes a secret casino; a rival lag turns out to be a secret snitch; a posh fraudster inmate teaches Harry to talk like an Etonian. Kevin finds his voice as a late-night prison-radio DJ.

Slowly, we watch Harry being hammered into the man that Conrad will later admire. By the final episode, he’s out, and ready to join the Paras.

I’ve made it no secret that Frankie Cooper is my favorite TV villain of 2026. But I’ll only accept a spin-off if Johnny Flynn stays in the role. So…

South London, the 1990s. Young Frankie Cooper is a gofer at a scrapyard, a well-manicured apprentice crook with an unfashionable hunger: he wants to know things. He enrolls in night classes, haunts the mobile library, and devours encyclopedias. Every lesson becomes a weapon. A lecture on Napoleon teaches him tactics. A documentary on parasites teaches him patience. Sun Tzu teaches him war.

Meanwhile he assembles his crew from the yard’s misfits: a weeping mechanic, a seven-foot pigeon fancier and a man who speaks solely in historical metaphors. Each week Frankie cons a bigger fish, charming the mark and leaving a trail of crushed-car cubes dripping blood. Beneath the bonhomie, we watch a very amiable monster being assembled, limb by limb, lesson by lesson, murder by murder.

Between all this, there's a love story—he meets the woman who will become Michael's mother. She's a nail technician from the posh part of Sydenham. She teaches him cuticle care, foot hygiene. Then she’s killed by a stray bullet in a shootout, leaving Frankie with a newborn son and a thirst for vengeance.

By the finale, the scrap yard is an empire. Then comes the one subject he can’t revise for: single fatherhood.

Ten years after MobLand, the Harrigans are all dead. Kat MacAllister won the war, salted the earth, and walked into the White House as president of the United States. But she never forgot that Harry turned her down. He survived the war, and lives off-grid on a remote Scottish isle, tending sheep and reading Plato. Once a month, he gets a postcard from Gina who is studying a master’s degree in Comparative Criminal Justice at The Hague.

One day, Harry receives a strange postcard from Gina, asking him to come. But something isn’t right. She signs off with “Your hugglepuff, Georgina.” He’s never called her Hugglepuff, and she always hated the name Georgina.

It’s a trap, and Harry knows it. Still, he dusts down his old leather coat and gets a ferry to the mainland. What follows is a race around Europe, from the Hebrides to Belgium to Washington DC, on one last job to save the only person he ever truly loved. On the journey he discovers that Gina’s criminology thesis accidentally unearthed a paper trail linking Kat's old MobLand war chest to the untraceable offshore funds that financed her presidential campaign.

Kat sees an opportunity. If she baits Harry out of the woodwork, she can frame him for a rogue, violent operation in Europe, eliminating both the evidence (Gina) and the last loose end from her past (Harry) in one clean stroke.

The hound is out of retirement. And, this time, it’s presidential.

This one charts Maeve’s humble beginnings in the wild west of Ireland. It follows her early years picking the pockets of drunk farmers at the Galway Races. At a dance, an amorous young man drags Maeve into a bush. What he hasn't considered is the razor blade she keeps in her petticoat. She cuts off his testicles.

She's the youngest and smallest–and by far the smartest—of a clan of bare-knuckle boxer brothers who never think to ask her to a fight.

So she watches and learns. She learns what men do when they think no one is watching, and what they’ll pay to keep it quiet. Her first scheme is small and sly. Her tenth ends with a bookie in a bog and the Irish mob on her tail.

She heads to London where she meets a young pickpocket with a bad mustache and a worse temper, and she sees the perfect partner. His name is Conrad Harrigan. Together they murder every last gangster with a grudge (and there are many).

It’s a love story, of a kind. Two outlaws, one getaway car, and a trail of widows in their rearview mirror.

#7036. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

#7030. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

AI security is entering a new stage as enterprise organizations look beyond protecting models from prompt injection, unauthorized access, and data exposure.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7029. Lauren Conrad’s Laguna Beach Mansion Lists for $26.5 Million—Plus 5 More Luxury Homes for Sale

For many Millennials, MTV’s Laguna Beach occupies a sacred place in the cultural canon. The reality show, which aired for three seasons in the mid-aughts, centered around an upscale Southern California enclave, where high school cool girl Lauren “L.C.” Conrad and her clique of telegenic friends had pool parties, bonfires—and plenty of drama. The backdrop for much of the series was Conrad’s opulent family residence, a blufftop Mediterranean-style compound designed by her architect father, James Conrad. Though the Conrads sold the 11-acre estate in 2008, it just hit the market again for the first time since then, priced at $26.5 million.

In other real estate news, the former home of screen legend Dorothy Dandridge, the first Black woman nominated for Best Actress at the Academy Awards, is now on the market for $5.5 million. And in New York’s Hudson Valley, the classical-infused retreat that ELLE Decor A-List architect Gil Schafer crafted for his own family is also available for $6.5 million. Check them all out below.

This house needs no introduction for fans of MTV’s hit reality show Laguna Beach. The six-bedroom mansion is where series star Lauren “L.C.” Conrad lived with her family, including dad James Conrad, the architect who designed the residence in 2004. Inside the 8,000-square-foot home are travertine floors, wood-beamed ceilings, and stone fireplaces, while the grounds come with an expansive pool court and sweeping Pacific Ocean vistas. When L.C. moved to Los Angeles to star in The Hills, her parents parted with the 11-acre estate, selling it for a reported $11.9 million in 2008. It’s now listed for $26.5 million with Sotheby’s broker Richard Trey Dewey.

ELLE Decor A-List talent Gil Schafer of the firm Schafer & Company is known for infusing stately homes with classical elegance, drawing from architecture styles of a bygone era to create residences that look like they’ve been standing for generations. For his own house in New York’s Hudson Valley, Schafer took cues from Greek Revival farmhouses to create a modern sanctuary with an old soul. Dubbed Middlefield, the 4,000-square-foot manor sits on 45 acres designed by another ELLE Decor A-Lister, landscape architect Deborah Nevins. Schafer parted with the property just last year for $6.18 million, nearly double his asking price. It’s now back on the market, listed with Ashley Whittaker of Compass for $6.5 million.

Trailblazing movie star Dorothy Dandridge made history as the first Black woman to be nominated for a Best Actress Oscar, for 1954’s Carmen Jones. Now, Ricarda Olander of Christie’s is selling Dandridge’s former Los Angeles residence for $5.5 million. The 1920s Mediterranean-style home spans roughly 4,400 square feet and comes with four bedrooms, five baths, and a series of outdoor areas designed for entertaining.

Perched atop a cast-iron SoHo landmark built in the 1870s, the glass-walled penthouse owned by soccer star Thierry Henry and model Andrea Rajacic just hit the market for $25 million. It’s the second time the couple has tried offloading the sprawling five-bedroom home, which they first put on the market in February 2025 for $26.75 million. Now listed with Sotheby’s broker Joshua Judge, the 5,600-square-foot condo has 4,600 square feet of private outdoor space thanks to a network of terraces across four different floors. Other perks include 14-foot ceilings, two deeded parking spaces, and an indoor bamboo garden.

From a one-bedroom cabin in rural Tennessee to a 63-acre Nashville compound, Dolly Parton’s homes reflected her humble beginnings and subsequent rise to the top of the entertainment industry. In the mid-1980s, a few years after the country music legend starred in her first Hollywood film, 9 to 5, she and her husband Carl Dean bought a property in Southern California. Now, just weeks after Parton’s death at age 80, their Los Angeles estate, which was rebuilt in 2009, according to the listing, just hit the market for $8 million.

Add this to your “Wi$h Li$t.” Taylor Swift’s former Beverly Hills bungalow just hit the market for nearly $8 million—and it’s brimming with pop star lore. Swift acquired the mini-compound in 2011, fresh off releasing her third album, Speak Now, when she was just 22 years old. At the time, she had a pair of Nashville residences to her name, though her property empire has since expanded exponentially.

#7027. John Cho’s Latest Role Took Him Back to the Life He Almost Lived

In his latest movie, the Harold & Kumar star saw something in the mirror: the man he almost was.

One of John Cho’s strongest childhood memories involves wearing a cowboy hat. Decades ago, just after the now 54-year-old actor had arrived from South Korea with his family, there was something called (to the best of Cho’s memory) “Western Heritage Day.” While it sounds like a bad hashtag cooked up by a far-right podcaster, the way the Harold & Kumar star describes it, it was just dress-up fun.

“All the kids had to come to school in a cowboy hat and boots. And my Korean mother was like, What?” Cho tells me. “I don’t know how, but she scrambled and got us a ten-gallon hat and boots.”

Years later, on the set of Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, Cho’s faint memory of rolling up to school all yee-haw and yee-howdy came flooding back. “When we did the fitting for Hwan, I was like, Oh, jeez. I’m doing this. This could have been me.”

For close to 30 years, John Cho has been one of the most consistently interesting—and reliable—actors in Hollywood. Since his 1997 debut in the bizarre indie comedy Shopping for Fangs (codirected by future Fast & Furious helmer Justin Lin), Cho has led a storied career in which he’s been finely tuned to the spirit of whatever story he was telling. Moviegoers first knew him in American Pie as a hormonal teenager who coined “MILF” and Frenched a photo of Jennifer Coolidge. Audiences have since seen him be serious (Searching), somber (Columbus), swoon worthy (the short-lived Selfie), and slick with swagger (Netflix’s Cowboy Bebop). With Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, Cho was determined to show off a different side of himself.

In the acclaimed action thriller by Bassam Tariq, Mahershala Ali stars as Latif, a hitman, widower, and devout Muslim desperate for breast milk to feed his infant child. Among the forces working against Latif is Hwan (Cho), a U.S. Army veteran and pastor whose ministry is armed to the teeth with Bibles and rifles—and they’re locked and loaded on Latif’s family and community.

“I saw it almost as a coming-of-age action story,” Cho observes. “A father who has to grow up in the absence of his wife, and his daughter who has to grow up in the absence of her mother, and how they find one another in that grief where the stakes are as high as they could be. It is an American story. It is filled with people trying to practice their faith as honestly as they can, and it’s hard sometimes.”

The chance to play a red-blooded American of Korean descent allowed the actor to imagine a life he might have had. Like Hwan, Cho lived in Houston before his family settled in Los Angeles. His father was a minister in the Church of Christ, and he encouraged John and his younger brother to assimilate into American culture to survive. Cho’s portrayal of Hwan is an actor wearing the boots of a man he almost was. “The exercise for me was: How could I, John Cho, who lived in Houston, have become that person?” he says. “All kinds of things happened that led me to where I am now, but how could I become that person? It was a fantasy for me as an actor to do all these things. I find [Hwan] to be so psychologically interesting.”

Below, Cho unloads on his latest turn as a villain for Bassam Tariq’s acclaimed festival feature, how he looks back on #StarringJohnCho ten years later, and future projects that may or may not include a long-awaited sequel to a millennial comedy classic.

This interview has been edited for clarity.

Take us to the beginning of Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother. Bassam Tariq told me before that he wanted to have you involved from the start. How did you come aboard?

There were three elements. The script, the filmmaker, and Mahershala Ali. I read the script first. It made me return to my experience as a first-time father. That feeling of breast milk as the most precious resource on earth—I was like, This is getting me to an anxious place. It was an incredibly fulfilling read. Then I watched Bassam’s first movie, Mogul Mowgli, and was so impressed. Then the question was: Can I hold my own against Mahershala? I’m such an admirer of his. My fears were allayed when we met. I knew he was going to take care of me. He’s a real leader and made sure I was gonna do my best work.

We just talked. My personal malfunction is that I like to talk in abstract all the time. It frustrates people in my family. Bassam’s like that too. I was like, Okay, I have a kindred spirit. His script led us to a wide-ranging discussion. He had to take a leap with me, because I don’t think any of us knew what that character was going to be based on the script. There were blanks we had to fill in together. I wasn’t sure how it was going to work, but I trusted my partners to make sure everything felt okay.

Talk to me about becoming Hwan. What drew him to you as an actor?

One thing Bassam and I agreed on was that I didn’t want this portrayal of this right-wing preacher to be a commentary on anyone. There’s a world in which we play him as a crook or a charlatan. For this to work, I wanted to play him authentically to what he’s saying. He really believes this. I was excited to explore how someone gets there. I’ve seen so many kinds of people walking down political paths I wasn’t even aware of. I’m like, How does someone get there? I have been thinking about it in my personal life a lot, [and this movie] was a chance to explore all that.

How did your childhood in Houston, raised by a minister father, help you understand Hwan and other ethnic-minority conservatives?

You’re right to assume, but I don’t see any crossover between Hwan’s beliefs and the Christianity I grew up with. There’s little crossover, except maybe their fervent belief that they’re doing the right thing. I knew people in the churches I grew up [who had conviction] in what they were doing. I wanted to bring that into [my performance].

I’ve always known that there’s more variation in people of color than most people understand. If you watch CNN, you just see voting blocs. It’s more fractured than that, particularly for Asian Americans, because the political situations in their home countries can inform how they lean. So it’s always been obvious to me, and I thought it was cool that it was clear to Bassam. He wanted to shine a light on it.

I know Hwan isn’t the first time you’ve played an antagonist. I point to your guest role on How I Met Your Mother as an early indicator that—

It showed your range for treachery! But after years of playing good guys, fathers, and romantic leads, what do you hope people see in you after wearing cowboy hats and preaching about God with guns?

I feel privileged to have had as many shots at this as I’ve had and to have a variety within that is really delightful to me. It’s hard to get the opportunity to do something different. I hope people appreciate it. That’s all.

You said just now that you played Hwan as someone who believes so strongly in what he preaches. By the end of the film, it looks like Hwan himself doesn’t know what he believes in anymore. What’s your take on Hwan at the very end?

I’m not sure what the day after is like for Hwan. I think we’ve all had this experience of having our worlds shook. Even when we receive information that what we believe is wrong, we tend to go back to what we feel comfortable. I don’t know whether Hwan was changed permanently. But he could have been. He saw something extraordinary.

Ten years ago, you were the face of #StarringJohnCho. How do you look back at that moment? What do you think it did for your career and for the industry?

It’s hard to understand what it did for me. It’s not like people sent me scripts. It was a clever, intelligent way to have a conversation I thought we needed to be having. Look at this poster. Why is this bizarre? Is it strange to you? Why? It’s hard to have a conversation like that these days in a way that’s not preachy. I thought it was a brilliant thing, I’m glad we had that conversation, and I’m glad to have been part of that.

Do you think the conversation around representation has moved forward, or has it regressed? I wonder if #StarringJohnCho could have happened now if it hadn’t happened then.

Back then, I thought we were past the monoculture. Looking back now, that seems so much closer to the monoculture than now. Every year, I think it’s impossible to have a big discussion, because our information diet is so much more fractured than it was six or 12 months ago. My inclination is that it’s just harder to have a single conversation with a lot of people now. But who knows? [Hashtag creator] Will Yu was someone who had digital literacy and knew how to talk to people. There’s gotta be someone around the corner who can do that. The idea is still a worthwhile one to bring up. We need the next Will Yu. Maybe we need Will Yu to answer again.

About five years ago, you starred in Netflix’s Cowboy Bebop, a huge live-action adaptation of an influential anime. How do you feel about how that show was received? Was there ever talk of a season 2?

There was talk of a season 2. I’m struggling to remember what decisions were made about the direction. But I look back on it fondly. We didn’t get season 2, but I wish we had. I felt like I got the melody down. I’d like to go off melody and play with it a little more. I wish we could have done that. We came at it from a very venerant place. We really liked and respected the material, and we tried to honor that as best we could. I don’t have regrets except that we didn’t get to do more.

More recently, you debuted on Korean television in the series Tempest last year. After a long career as a Korean American in Hollywood, what culture shock did you experience working as a Hollywood actor in Korea?

I was so curious what that experience would be like. I, selfishly, just wanted to know what that was and if that was a real path to doing stuff [there]. There are differences that are significant, but the commonalities outweigh the differences. It’s mostly linguistic and cultural stuff. The talent level is so high. I’m optimistic we’ll see more of these coproductions. And personally, having been born in Korea, it was cool. I brought my family. We spent the summer, and it deepened the experience.

Your next project is something called The Off Weeks. What can you tell me about it?

It’s an Apple TV series. It stars Jessica Chastain, Ben Stiller, and Richard Gere. It’s a mystery, a comedy, a thriller. The attraction for me was to work with that cast, and I’m excited for you to see it. It’s bananas. I worked with all those actors, and they were all very different and all thrilling.

It’s been a long time since audiences have seen Harold and Kumar. I know a continuation has been thrown around for a long time. What’s the latest word?

There are people who are trying to give the people what they want. We’ll see if we can do it. It might be a good thing to make a Harold & Kumar 4. That’s all I’ll say.

#7023. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7019. Three Ways to Master the Noir Aesthetic

Dior’s American Psycho pieces bring a dark cinematic edge to modern menswear.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Film noir has given menswear some of its most enduring images: the broad-shouldered suit, the long overcoat, the shirt with its collar open just enough to suggest trouble. The clothes are sharp, the colors run dark, and the men wearing them tend to look like they've got somewhere important to be. Even when they don't.

You don't need a private investigator's wardrobe to borrow from the genre. A well-cut suit will get you most of the way there, while a graphic T-shirt or a statement bag can bring the look into the present. Dior's American Psycho T-shirt and Book Tote offer a particularly good entry point, drawing on the cover art of Bret Easton Ellis's novel for a dose of dark literary and cinematic attitude. Here's how to make the aesthetic work in three different ways.

The suit is the natural starting point. Classic noir leads tend to wear clothes with presence: broad shoulders, defined waists, full-length trousers, and jackets with enough structure to hold their shape. You can take those proportions as inspiration without dressing like you've wandered off a 1940s soundstage.

Start with a charcoal or black suit, a white or pale blue shirt, and black leather shoes. A long wool overcoat adds another layer and gives the silhouette a little drama. Pay attention to fit, particularly through the shoulders and trouser leg. The goal is a clean line from top to bottom, not a suit so tight you can't sit through dinner.

For a more relaxed interpretation, replace the dress shirt with a fine-gauge black knit or wear the jacket with dark, straight-leg trousers. The result retains the polish of classic Hollywood while feeling appropriate for a modern wardrobe. Save the fedora for a particularly committed evening.

Film noir has always had a strong visual identity. A graphic T-shirt lets you reference that world without committing to a full suit. Dior's black American Psycho tee, which features embroidered artwork based on the first-edition cover of Bret Easton Ellis's notorious novel, brings a literary reference to a familiar menswear staple.

The trick is to keep the rest of the outfit simple. Wear the shirt with charcoal trousers, black jeans, or a pair of dark, pleated wool pants. Add a structured blazer if you want to sharpen things up, or wear it on its own with leather boots for a more casual look. Either way, let the graphic take center stage.

There's a good bit of irony in wearing American Psycho on your chest. Patrick Bateman, the novel's image-obsessed protagonist, treats clothes as a measure of status and a way to distinguish himself from everyone around him. The reference has some bite, particularly when paired with understated basics rather than an outfit that tries to do too much. You get a little cinematic attitude without looking like you're headed to a costume party.

A bag can change the character of an outfit, especially when the rest of the clothes are fairly restrained. The Large Book Tote features the same book-cover artwork as the matching tee. It has a practical shape, with room for a 15-inch laptop and everyday essentials, and can be carried by hand or over the shoulder.

The graphic gives the bag a personality of its own, so there's no need to build an entire outfit around it. Carry it with a long charcoal overcoat, a dark crewneck sweater, straight-leg trousers, and polished loafers. The combination nods to noir's shadowy palette while the bag adds a more contemporary point of interest. You don't need to dress like a private eye to make the reference land. A few well-chosen pieces will do the job.

Start with a dark suit, tailored trousers, a long overcoat, and polished leather shoes. For a more casual take, pair a graphic T-shirt with dark denim or pleated trousers. Keep the silhouette sharp and the palette restrained.

Mix classic tailoring with contemporary pieces. A fine-gauge knit can replace a dress shirt, while a graphic tee or statement bag adds personality to an otherwise understated outfit. The goal is to borrow from film noir's visual language without dressing for a period drama.

Black, charcoal, gray, navy, and white form the foundation of the look. High contrast, such as a pale shirt against a dark suit, is especially effective. You can also introduce muted brown or olive for a softer take that still feels true to the aesthetic.

Film noir style tends toward dark palettes, sharp tailoring, and an understated, slightly brooding mood. Old Hollywood style is a broader category that includes everything from formal tuxedos to relaxed studio-era tailoring. The two overlap, but film noir offers a more specific and shadowy approach to dressing.

#7018. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

AI security is entering a new stage as enterprise organizations look beyond protecting models from prompt injection, unauthorized access, and data exposure.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7015. Marshals Is Ruining the Dutton Family Legacy

The Yellowstone spinoff wants to move on from its Kevin Costner-sized shadow, but it can’t.

Ever since John Dutton (Kevin Costner) died, the Yellowstone-verse has sought to do away with everything he stood for. Without the patriarch in charge, it’s dawned on his children, Kayce (Luke Grimes) and Beth (Kelly Reilly), that the man was too violent and too stubborn. So, Kayce sold the ranch and resolved to put his violent life behind in the spinoff, Marshals, while Beth moved to Texas in Dutton Ranch to try ranching her own way.

It’s not really working out. Beth and her husband Rip (Cole Hauser) basically just moved the whole Dutton operation to Texas. And as much as Kayce’s plan to turn his last remaining acres of the Yellowstone Ranch into an equine therapy center would follow through on his plan for peace, he’s too busy continuing his father’s cycle of violence by running into danger as a member of a team of U.S. Marshals every week. Why? Well, because peace for the Duttons just isn’t good TV.

Still, Marshals tries its best to weave the framework into season 2. The latest round of episodes to kick off the second season deals heavily with post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), and the various ways the Marshals team handles their crazy, violent lives. Mostly, it’s by bottling it up and never speaking about it. After killing a man in the season 1 finale, Kayce’s son Tate (Brecken Merrill) says a line in season 2, episode 3 that could have been plucked directly from a Dutton family quote book: “What happened stays where it is.”

But try as they might, the Dutton children are becoming their father anyway. By now in the task force spinoff, Kayce’s likely killed more men on the ranch than his father ever did. And though he’s not branding people like cattle anymore—which still haunts Kayce’s dreams in season 2, episode 4—even John Dutton gave up that practice after Yellowstone season 3.

Most likely, the Marshals team just wrote themselves into a trap. They might think they’re providing Kayce good backstory by tackling his inner quest for a peaceful life, but when he heads to work to kill criminals every morning, you can’t really believe the guy. Is he trying at all? And since he’s clearly not, maybe we should focus on that instead...?

So, at the end of episode 3, Kayce demands that Tate break his family’s generational cycle and take his ass to therapy. “It’s the last place my father would’ve taken me, and the last place I would’ve gone myself,” he says.

Two episodes have passed since that moment, and we haven’t seen Tate actually go yet. Plus, Kayce hasn’t exactly set a good example. Despite ordering his son to open up, Kayce didn’t give us any hints that he’s decided to see a therapist himself. And that equine center? We’ll be lucky if we ever see it until Marshals is over for good.

#7013. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

AI security is entering a new stage as enterprise organizations look beyond protecting models from prompt injection, unauthorized access, and data exposure.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

#7010. SNL’s You Can See Everything Parody with Druski Resurrects a Classic Eddie Murphy Bit

Back in 1984, Eddie Murphy had fun with John Howard Griffin’s 1961 book Black Like Me with a Saturday Night Live sketch titled White Like Me. In it, Murphy caked on makeup to pass as a white man, flipping his world upside down. It’s still pretty funny to watch after all these years, even if putting on makeup to resemble another race for a joke is walking a tightrope. So it’s damn impressive that Saturday Night Live just got away with it again, with Druski.

On the most recent Saturday Night Live, guest star Druski was at the center of one of the night’s best sketches (and maybe the only one worth watching), which was also the inevitable parody of You Can See Everything, titled Dru Can See Everything.

The premise is simple: What if, before Nathan Fielder, Druski first filmed the final days of freedom for Elizabeth Holmes? Cast members Ben Marshall and an incredible Jane Wickline co-star and elevate the sketch from a mildly funny “haha” to “holy shit they’re actually committed here.” Clocking in just four minutes long, the sketch runs through the highlights of You Can See Everything’s first hour: Elizabeth (Wickline) and her partner Billy (Marshall) living at home, cooking eggs, showing off the controversial Theranos machine, and even Billy’s barenaked shower. But the one change is swapping out Fielder for Druski. And it makes a huge difference.

You can see everything (the sketch) below.

While Fielder’s social awkwardness and determination to ensnare a charlatan without falling victim himself is part of the movie’s drama, SNL knows that Druski would just call out Holmes and Evans’s bullshit straight up. No hesitation. “I just want people to see me as an ordinary person. Can you do that, Druski?” asks Wickline’s Elizabeth Holmes.

Says Druski, “Based on what I’ve seen so far, I’mma go ahead and say no. Y’all got some of the weirdest, freakiest vibe I’ve ever seen.” Grabbing the bowl of eggs, he then turns to the camera: “Whatever they said this bitch did, she done did that sh-t.” (Remember the eggs scene is like, ten minutes into the movie.)

Funny as Druski is, the real MVPs are Wickline and Marshall, even if all their jokes are about the size of Elizabeth and Billy’s saucer-pan eyes. The now spoofed-to-death “I’m always real” scene gets its SNL roast too, with Druski asking literally point-blank, “Are you trying to f-ck me?” Then, when Elizabeth shows off Theranos’s supposedly miraculous machine, Druski doesn’t hold back. “Listen white girl, I’mma say this with all love, right? This the most Temu ass piece of sh-t I done ever seen in my life.” Cut to “Billy” and his weird lizard.

(By the way, we know Saturday Night Live’s production design is top-tier, but it’s frankly amazing they were able to build a virtually precise replica of Holmes’s oceanfront rental and recreate the lighting conditions to make the shots look passably real. Remember that they shoot these things in under a week. I demand a behind-the-scenes video of how they pulled this one off.)

The sketch peaks at the end, when Druski emulates Fielder’s method for staging reenactments by putting on White Chicks-style makeup (to better understand Elizabeth, of course). There’s no getting around it: the man looks freaky. Uncanny. He’s like Fiona from Shrek in a J. Crew sweater. Which makes the joke’s reveal a funny jump scare. Post-midterms, it’s honestly awesome that this kind of woke comedy is “back” (if it actually ever left). I’m not smart enough to suss out if whiteface is actually acceptable. I just know Druski and the SNL team killed it this week.

#7008. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#7003. The 12 Best Puffer Jackets You Can Buy Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We love a barn jacket. We love a blazer. But when it’s below freezing and snowing sideways, neither is going to do you much good. That’s puffer jacket weather, and no number of layered sweaters under your favorite jacket is going to change the forecast.

Luckily, there’s no shortage of puffer jackets out there. The trick is finding one that’s warm enough, durable enough, and ready to stand up to whatever precipitation the winter sky throws its way—all while looking good enough that you don’t feel like the Michelin Tire Man every time you leave the house. The good news? There are plenty of puffer jackets that fit the bill, from luxury options from Canada Goose and Moncler to more affordable ones from REI and Carhartt.

There is a range in how exactly a puffer gets its shape, too. Most are down-filled, and the way the stitching is designed affects the puffiness. The fewer the stitches, the bigger the puffs. For something more packed down, there are ones with denser stitching that make for a slimmer-fitting jacket. Then you get into the nitty-gritty: Is the hood removable? Can you wash it at home? Is it really, truly, waterproof? Start your shopping with these puffer jackets you can buy right now and wear for many cold days to come.

One of the most iconic purveyors of the puffer jacket, TNF is a brand that deserves the hype it gets. This style, with its wide puffs, is a throwback look that’s oh so welcome in the 2020s. It's designed to tough out even the coldest winter days, with a water-repellent and windproof outer, goose down, and a three-piece hood that you can stow away into the collar.

To me, this is the perfect packable puffer: Slim enough to roll up and stash away and sturdy enough to brave wind, rain, and snow. REI designs this lightweight style for the man on the go, so for all your winter hikes and outdoor excursions, this is the perfect in-between option that won't weigh you down.

Canada Goose is a household name when it comes to winter gear, and the Lawrence Puffer is one of its best sellers for good reason. Our editors (along with actors like Daniel Craig and Ethan Hawke) love this lightweight, warm, cozy jacket, which was designed with the details in mind; it has a suede chin guard so the stand collar won’t bother you, backpack straps, reflective stripes, and even two different color options for the disc logo.

Eddie Bauer’s CirrusLite is a sleeper hit. It won’t win awards as the most wind-resistant or coolest looking, though I love the more earthy colorways. It layers well, keeps you warm, and is durable enough if you need to wear it on a legit mountain—all this while being super lightweight and easy to pack.

Whether you're commuting in a bustling city or packing for a cold-weather trip, this lightweight jacket is a winter staple. It's stylish but doesn't skimp on insulation for a chilly day. The best part, though: Unlike many other puffers, this one is about as slim fitting as you can get. Kiss all the extra bulk goodbye this winter. Or, if temperatures go below freezing, add a wool coat on top for extra coverage.

Not only is this jacket insulated, but the interior is lined with sherpa, too, making it a cold-weather superhero. The high collar and full-front zip make it virtually windproof, so whether you’re darting from building to building or staying outside for a while, you’re all set.

Don’t get me wrong, an Arc’teryx Thorium is a phenomenal jacket. I just think at that $450 price point, you’re often just as good going for a North Face Nuptse (if you want an outer layer) or an REI down jacket (for layering). I think Veilance, which is the higher-end and more fashionable Arc’teryx sub-label, is a more interesting proposition. You get a sleek city cut, a nicer matte ripstop shell, and insanely warm 1000-fill down.

Rocky Mountain makes one of the sickest jackets out there. Westernwear is on a bit of a downturn—well, it’s classic, but had its cultural peak two years ago—but you are seeing this style of working Western down jacket with leather yoke pop up more and more. Rocky Mountain Featherbed is the OG, starting in the 1960s as a real brand for cattlemen and their employees. Its current iteration is actually a Japanese-owned reproduction, which means it’s the real fucking deal. It looks cool, wears hard, and is probably warmer than anything on this list.

Lululemon is based in Canada, and maybe that explains why all of its outerwear is so good. We love its bulkier puffer jacket, but for a sleeker, winter athleisure feel, this minimally puffed jacket is great, too. It keeps the heat in and water out, and with a stretchy, down-filled construction, you’re sure to stay warm and cozy.

This puffer from Aritzia was made with the same attention to detail and tech that goes into high-performance sleeping bags. It has 700-fill down, but it’s sewn with big horizontal chambers like a sleeping bag. It’ll withstand temperatures as low as -22°F, so it’s the only coat you’ll need for surviving a serious cold snap. The fit is intentionally oversized—longer in the arms and broader at the shoulders—to accommodate bulky sweaters underneath. Plus, the Japanese fabric is both water-repellent and wind-resistant.

Is this an outdoorsy jacket? No. Is it outdoors capable? Yes, absolutely. Heavy down jackets—as real mountaineers use them—are made for base camp, when you’re not moving but need a lot of heat. This is perfect for that.

The issue with most puffer jackets is that they’re not made to be especially abrasion or weather resistant. These jackets were originally made for cold and dry days on mountaintops. But Filson takes a different approach.

The Down Cruiser shell is a classic heavy oil-finished Filson cotton. That makes this jacket more weather-resistant and abrasion-resistant than any other option on this list.

So, you want the best? For performance, you can buy something cheaper from an outdoors brand. For fashion, you can get something cool and garment-dyed from Stone Island. For a merging of those sensibilities, Moncler is the only brand—the OG that made jackets for Europe’s mountaineers—that you should be buying from.

The Grenoble name denotes Moncler’s high-end ski line. Go to a nice ski resort in the Alps, and you’ll see plenty of these. Nothing looks as good or insulates as well. And if you don’t ski but want something fashion-forward, I still prefer the Grenoble line. The abrasion-resistant fabrics on the jackets look a lot better than the high-shine nylon that Moncler and other brands like to do on puffers.

While finding the perfect puffer jacket isn’t rocket science, there are a few key features you should focus on to narrow your search down from the 20 options above.

Insulation: Some climates call for the most intense down fill power there is, and some simply don’t. You don’t want to overheat this winter, nor do you want to be too cold, so go for a puffer with fill or insulation that your climate calls for. Don’t make the mistake of buying an 850-fill-power puffer if you live in a city where the coldest it ever gets is, like, 59 degrees.

Features: Not all puffer jackets are made the same. Personally, I need a removable hood. Look for design features including adjustability at the hem and hood, windproof fabric, zippered or buttoned pockets, a mock neck, and more, depending on your preferences.

Puffiness: If you’re someone who travels a lot and needs to stash your puffer in a suitcase from time to time, going for a super-puffy option probably isn’t going to be your best bet. Instead, look for puffers with a little less, well, puff, which will make them much easier to stow and pack. Some on this list even come with their own stow bags.

Being based in New York City means we know what makes a good puffer jacket. The options we listed above bring the heat in terms of functionality and style, all season long. Over the years, our editors have tried hundreds of styles and brands. Narrowing it down to our 20 favorites was no small feat, but these are just the picks that we swear by and return to time and time again for our winter wear.

Fill power is the number that tells you the quality of down in your jacket. The number technically indicates how many cubic inches of loft a single ounce of down has, but for simplicity, just focus on whether the number is relatively high or low—the higher it is, the higher-quality the insulation is: 600 is good, 700 is great, and 800 is spectacular.

Over time, as down goes through washing machines, tough weather conditions, and daily life, it can lose its fluffiness and slightly deflate your puffer. It’ll still keep you warm and dry, sure, just not quite so puffy.

#7000. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.
