#6996. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6989. How 12 Famous Watches Got Their Nicknames

From the 'Steve McQueen' Rolex to the 'Holy Grail' Omega.

A few weeks ago, Casio unveiled a new range of fully analogue G-Shock watches, billed as its biggest launch in decades. But what immediately caught collectors' imaginations wasn't the technology inside the new 2500 Series. It was the shape of the cases, whose rounded rectangular outlines bear more than a passing resemblance to the Patek Philippe Nautilus.

There’s precedent here. When Casio’s eight-sided GA-2100 appeared in 2019, enthusiasts immediately clocked its likeness to Audemars Piguet's Royal Oak and christened it the 'CasiOak'. The nickname stuck and has since become shorthand for the entire family. (Perhaps this is what happens when you launch watches with names like GA-2100.)

So what to call the new Nautilus lookalike? Suggestions have included “Nauty-Less” and “CasiNau,” but “CasioNaut” seems to have won out.

Elsewhere in recent watch news, Seiko has been having fun with another nickname. Most watch fans will be familiar with the term “panda,” used to describe a chronograph with a white main dial and two black sub-dials, an arrangement said to resemble the face of the bamboo-munching bear. Seiko's new Power Design Project Panda takes the resemblance to its logical conclusion, with actual panda eyes, ears and, even a little wagging tail on the case.

And next week, Piaget will release a new version of its Andy Warhol watch, a name collectors had been using for decades before the brand finally made it official in 2024, following an agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation.

Clearly, the peculiar business of giving watches nicknames is alive and well. Which gives us an excuse, as if one were needed, to run through a dozen great watch nicknames and the stories behind them.

Some are named after astronauts, actors, and models. Others have more unusual stories, involving a collaboration that never happened, a celebrity who almost certainly never wore the watch named after him, and an American president who wasn't even allowed to accept his watch.

Piaget ref. 15102 and its modern descendants

In the 1970s, Piaget was making some of the most extravagant watches money could buy, with chunky gold cases, colorful stone dials, and an impressive list of celebrity customers. Among them was Andy Warhol, who owned at least seven Piagets, including a large, cushion-shaped gold model, reference 15102, which he bought in 1973. (Four of his watches are now in Piaget’s collection.)

Six years later, Warhol met Yves Piaget, the founder’s great-grandson, and the two became friends. Warhol joined the Piaget Society, the glamorous circle of artists, designers, and celebrities cultivated by the company.

Introduced in 1972, the 15102 was produced for just five years before disappearing from Piaget’s catalogue. The design returned in 2014 under the name Black Tie, although collectors had long since nicknamed the original the “Andy Warhol.” Ten years later, the company finally made the name official, signing a licensing agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation for the Visual Arts in 2024.

Piaget has since developed a collection bearing the artist’s name, with designs that draw on his taste for unconventional colors and materials. Among the first was a Clou de Paris edition featuring a blue meteorite dial surrounded by rows of tiny gold pyramids.

Warhol’s association has done vintage prices no harm, either. In May this year, an example resembling his own sold at Sotheby’s in Geneva for CHF166,399 – about $200,000.

Omega Speedmaster ref. 311.30.42.30.01.004

The Omega Speedmaster “Tintin” takes its name from a collaboration with the Belgian cartoon character that never quite happened.

In 2013, Omega released a version of its Speedmaster Professional chronograph (officially called the Racing) with an unusual red-and-white checkered pattern around the edge of its black dial. It was inspired by the rocket in Hergé’s Tintin adventures Destination Moon and Explorers on the Moon.

The original plan was to produce the watch in partnership with the owners of the Tintin rights. Early prototypes even featured the rocket on the dial, with a special case back to match. But the deal fell through, and Omega went ahead without the Tintin branding, leaving a rather curious design detail whose inspiration the company couldn't openly celebrate.

The story came to light when Robert-Jan Broer, founder of the watch website Fratello, spoke to Omega’s head of product development. Once collectors understood the connection, the “Tintin” nickname stuck. Illustrations from the comics accompanying Fratello’s coverage were subsequently removed at the request of the rights holder.

That said, the owners weren't opposed to putting the character on a watch altogether. Omega’s Swatch Group stablemate Swatch had already produced two official Tintin models for the character’s 75th anniversary in 2004, including a Happy Birthday Tintin edition limited to 9,999 pieces. Omega simply never managed to get its own collaboration over the line.

Seiko 6139, particularly the yellow-dial 6139-6005

Omega may have the Moonwatch, but Seiko can lay claim to the first automatic chronograph worn in space. And unlike the Omegas issued by Nasa, the Seiko in question was bought by the astronaut himself.

In June 1972, US Air Force pilot William Pogue paid $71 for a yellow-dial Seiko 6139 chronograph. He was training for NASA’s Skylab program and needed a watch to time engine burns. Like other astronauts, Pogue was due to receive an Omega Speedmaster, the chronograph Nasa had qualified for spaceflight, but his hadn't yet been delivered.

By the time his Omega arrived, however, Pogue had become so attached to the Seiko that he decided to take it into space as well.

When Skylab 4 launched in November 1973, the Seiko travelled in a pocket of Pogue’s spacesuit. During the mission’s 84 days in orbit, he wore it on his left wrist, with the Omega on his right. (The Seiko remained set to US Central Time.)

For years, the Seiko’s part in space history went largely unknown. Then American watch collector David Bruno spotted a reference to it in a mission transcript and contacted Pogue to find out what he'd worn. In 2008, the astronaut consigned the watch through the Astronaut Scholarship Foundation. It sold at Heritage Auctions for a surprisingly modest $5,975, complete with its original receipt and Pogue’s handwritten account.

The original Seiko 6139 has long been discontinued, but yellow-dial examples are now widely known as “Pogues.” They're still relatively affordable, too. Seiko has since revisited the look with the solar-powered Prospex SSC947, but it's an interpretation rather than a direct reissue.

There is one problem with the Rolex Explorer II colloquially known as the “Steve McQueen.” Nobody has been able to establish that Steve McQueen ever wore one.

His Rolex of choice was a Submariner. Yet the Explorer II association has become so firmly established that even Sotheby’s used the nickname when offering an example in May 2022, while the description below acknowledged that no convincing evidence existed for the connection. (Who first decided to attach the actor’s name to the watch remains a mystery.)

In fact, the same model has another nickname with an easier-to-fathom explanation. Freccione is Italian for “big arrow,” referring to the whopping orange 24-hour hand that dominates its dial.

Introduced in 1971, the Explorer II was designed for cave explorers and others who might spend extended periods without natural daylight. Its extra hand completed one revolution every 24 hours, allowing the wearer to distinguish day from night.

With its fixed steel bezel, busy dial, and that unmistakable orange hand, the original Explorer II looks quite unlike most of Rolex’s better-known models. It has since become a firm favorite with collectors.

In 1987, Omega introduced a Speedmaster that put a new spin on the familiar Moonwatch. The ref. 376.0822 featured an automatic movement, a day and date display, a 24-hour indicator, and an additional hand to count elapsed minutes. It made for a busy, rather technical-looking watch, and one that wasn't destined for a long production run.

Its relative scarcity would eventually catch the attention of American collector and Omega expert Chuck Maddox.

In 1999, Maddox was offered a 376.0822 in Germany for around $600. At roughly the same time, another Speedmaster appeared for sale in Iowa at a similar price. Faced with a choice, he went for the Iowa watch, reasoning that dealing with a seller he could visit in person was less risky than buying from overseas.

Almost immediately, he regretted passing up the German example. Finding another proved far more difficult than he had expected. A watch appeared in Japan with a price tag of more than $10,000. Another opportunity slipped away at auction. Eventually, with help from a fellow enthusiast, he managed to track one down.

Maddox later wrote about his hunt on his website and in online watch forums, comparing his obsession with finding the elusive Speedmaster to King Arthur’s search for the Holy Grail. (Watch collectors have never knowingly undersold a story.) He began referring to the watch simply as “the Grail,” and the nickname caught on among fellow collectors.

It's a tale that will be painfully familiar to anyone who's ever collected watches: the one that got away, and the lengths you'll go to for a second chance to spend a load of money.

When quartz technology arrived in the late 1960s, its promise was greater accuracy and, eventually, affordable watches for almost everyone. Rolex, naturally, took a different path.

Its first quartz watch, launched in 1970, was available exclusively in gold. The white-gold version of the ref. 5100 cost 18,300 Swiss francs before tax. According to Rolex expert James Dowling, that was roughly the price of a Porsche 912 and two-and-a-half times what the company charged for a Day-Date on its President bracelet.

Inside was the Beta 21, an early Swiss quartz movement developed jointly by a consortium of manufacturers. The technology was remarkable enough, but the watch looked like no Rolex that had come before it. Its large, angular case, broad bezel and integrated gold bracelet were a world away from the familiar designs of the period.

Italian collectors took to calling it the “Texano,” or “Texan,” presumably in recognition of its considerable size and extravagance.

Only around 1,000 numbered examples are thought to have been made, mostly in yellow gold. More than half a century later, the “Texano” remains as rare and extravagant as ever.

Nothing to do with glamorous magazine models, as it happens. The nickname comes from a book published in 2009 by Zenith historian and collector Manfred Rössler, who chose a photograph of the El Primero A3818 for the cover of Zenith: Swiss Watch Manufacture Since 1865. His reason for picking that particular watch? He simply liked blue dials. Fair enough.

Introduced in 1971, the A3818 was a 37mm steel chronograph with an angular case housing Zenith's celebrated El Primero movement. Its vertically brushed blue dial featured contrasting sub-dials and distinctive shark's-tooth markings around the edge.

Only 1,000 examples were made, according to Zenith, and the model had long since disappeared from production when Rössler's book brought it back to collectors' attention. Zenith revived the design in 2020, with a lightweight titanium Airweight edition following in 2022. Both had “Cover Girl” as part of their official names.

It doesn't get much more ’60s than Finnish fashion model Nina Rindt, married to Austrian Formula One driver Jochen Rindt and a regular fixture in the paddock during the sport's glamorous heyday. Photographs from the late 1960s show her watching races from the pit lane, often wearing a white-dial Universal Genève Compax chronograph on a wide leather cuff. The combination of glamorous wearer, racing connection, and period photographs proved irresistible to watch collectors, who began calling the model the “Nina Rindt.” Its black-dial counterpart acquired the even better moniker “Evil Nina.”

The nicknames helped transform the watch's fortunes. In the early 2010s, as collectors became increasingly interested in vintage chronographs with famous associations, prices for the “Nina Rindt” rose dramatically. As Eric Wind, founder of Wind Vintage and a former Christie's watch specialist, recalled: “That watch went from $3,000 ordinarily and was worth $40,000 within a year.” One particularly fine example sold at Christie's in 2015 for $47,500.

When Universal Genève relaunched in April this year, the Compax was central to its comeback, with new chronographs inspired by both versions. The watches now have automatic movements but retain the contrasting sub-dials of the originals—and are even offered on the wide leather cuff that helped make Nina's watch so recognizable.

In the 1983 film Octopussy, Roger Moore's James Bond wore a Seiko G757 Sports 100 that had been given a special upgrade by Q Branch. Its small circular digital display functioned as a tracking screen, allowing Bond to follow a transmitter concealed inside a Fabergé egg being sought by the film's villain, Kamal Khan.

Almost 30 years later, in 2012, Casio introduced the AE-1200, an inexpensive digital world timer with a striking resemblance to Bond's gadget. It had a similarly angular case, multiple digital displays and, most importantly, a little circular clock that seemed to recall the tracking screen on Moore's Seiko.

Enthusiasts spotted the resemblance and came up with a pun on Casino Royale, the 2006 Bond film starring Daniel Craig. Thus the “Casio Royale” was born, a nickname involving a different watch, a different manufacturer, and an entirely different Bond film.

The AE-1200 remains in Casio's catalog today, costing $55 and looking much as it did in 2012. Its miniature world map indicates the selected time zone, while the little circular display shows the time. It also has five alarms, a stopwatch, and a countdown timer. The Fabergé egg tracker is not included.

By 1972, Bulova was advertising the water resistance of its Oceanographer dive watch with the words “666 FEET” printed prominently on the dial. That's approximately 203 metres, although quite why the company chose such an oddly specific figure rather than a round 200 meters isn't entirely clear.

Given the number's rather ominous biblical associations, it didn't take long for enthusiasts to christen the watch the alliteratively pleasing “Devil Diver.”

The Oceanographer had other things going for it, too. The original was a handsome example of early-1970s dive-watch design, with a substantial cushion-shaped case, a rotating bezel, and a dial with extravagantly bold markers intended to be easily read underwater.

In 2017, Bulova teamed up with New York vintage-watch dealer Analog/Shift to gauge interest in reviving one of its archive designs. The Oceanographer came out on top. A memorable nickname with a whiff of danger presumably didn't hurt.

The watch duly returned in 2018, including an orange-dial edition limited to, naturally, 666 pieces. Since then, Bulova has produced smaller versions and an Oceanographer GMT capable of tracking a second time zone.

More than 50 years later, Bulova still prints “666 FEET” on the dial, although its official specifications now give the water resistance as a rather neater 200 meters.

Audemars Piguet Royal Oak Offshore ref. 25721

“You have spoilt my watch, you're a murderer!”

According to designer Emmanuel Gueit, this was Gérald Genta's reaction when he first saw the Royal Oak Offshore, a considerably beefier version of the Royal Oak he had designed for Audemars Piguet in 1972.

AP had been hoping to recapture some of the impact of the original Royal Oak, which had caused a sensation with its unusually large steel case, integrated bracelet, and extravagant price. By the early 1990s, the company wanted something to appeal to a younger audience, and Gueit was given the job.

His answer, launched in 1993, was the Royal Oak Offshore. The original's 39mm case grew to 42mm, enormous by the standards of the day. It was thicker, too, with a chronograph movement, rubber-coated crown and pushers, and a conspicuous gasket visible beneath the bezel. The Offshore name came from powerboat racing, a suitably muscular association for such an imposing watch.

The industry quickly christened it “The Beast.” Genta himself reportedly compared it to a sea elephant, which, frankly, would have made an even better nickname.

Even within Audemars Piguet, enthusiasm was mixed. The company's archive records that some insiders hoped the project would fail, while the first hundred examples had only Royal Oak engraved on their case backs, without the Offshore name. Hardly a ringing vote of confidence.

Customers eventually came around, helped by celebrity associations, particularly with Arnold Schwarzenegger. The Offshore went on to help establish the fashion for oversized luxury watches, which must have had Genta sobbing even harder into his fondue.

Omega Speedmaster Apollo XI ref. BA145.022-69

When Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin landed on the moon in July 1969, Omega had good reason to celebrate. Its Speedmaster chronographs had accompanied NASA astronauts throughout the Apollo program, and the company decided to mark the achievement by making 1,014 special gold editions.

These were considerably more luxurious than the steel watches issued to the astronauts, with 18-carat yellow-gold cases, bracelets, and dials, plus burgundy bezels. The first two were intended for President Richard Nixon and Vice President Spiro Agnew, complete with individual engravings.

There was just one problem. American rules governing gifts to public officials meant neither man could accept an expensive gold watch from a Swiss manufacturer. Both were returned to Omega, where they remain in the company's museum in Switzerland today.

Other recipients were luckier. Astronauts received their watches at a gala dinner in Houston in November 1969, while most of the remaining production was offered for public sale. Collectors subsequently nicknamed the reference the “Nixon,” after the president who never got to wear his.

The watches have since become highly collectible, particularly those with a connection to the Apollo astronauts. An example sold at Bonhams in 2024 for HK$384,000 [just under $50K], while Neil Armstrong's own gold Speedmaster, number 17, fetched $2.125 million in April last year.

Of course, Omega couldn't resist reviving the design, producing another 1,014 examples in its Moonshine gold alloy in 2019. But the originals have an appeal that can never quite be replicated, combining as they do mid-century space-age glamour with a whiff of Nixonian roguishness.

#6985. Tom Hardy’s Hair Through The Years—and How to Get Every Cut

Three decades, one head, a lot of styles. We’ll help you nail every look with advice from the pros.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You can track Tom Hardy’s life through his haircuts. The floppy, past-the-ears noughties look. The bald Bane era. His Peaky Blinders slick-back. Then there’s the fade he wore on The Revenant press tour and the short-back-and-sides he’s settled into today. He’s been on a proper hair journey over the past couple of decades and, somehow, he’s looked great on every stop along the way.

Really, he’s done exactly what every man should do when it comes to hair. You should never have the same haircut at 60 as when you’re 16, so changing it up is essential. Sure, some, for Hardy, were for his roles. He probably wouldn’t have gone for the full shaved look if he hadn’t been cast as a masked supervillain who breaks Batman’s back. But, even so, his go-to now is a long way from the boyband look he wore when he was younger.

So, we asked some of London’s best barbers how to get each cut, as well as who each one suits. If you’re anything like Hardy, you’ll suit all six.

The haircut: Think of this as the slick-back’s more laid-back cousin. “It's a classic textured scissor cut with a low, natural finish,” says Robyn Mulgrew, a senior barber at Ruffians. “It’s kept soft around the ears and nape, with the sides relatively full and enough length to sweep it back.” No harsh fades or sharp lines, just a cut that looks like it grew naturally—and it’s a versatile shape that works for everything from oval to square and round faces.

What to ask for: Mulgrew’s advice? Keep it simple: “Don’t overcomplicate it, or you’ll confuse each other,” she says. “Use these key words: textured scissor cut with a low natural taper, swept-back top, styled to give a soft, lived-in finish.” All you need to do now is read it out with the help of a Hardy reference photo—job done.

How to style it: The idea is for your hair to look naturally lifted rather than quiffed. “I'd prep with Ruffians Beach Salt Spray, then blow-dry it in, sweeping the hair back and up,” says Mulgrew. “Keep the sides flatter so it still looks smart, then finish with out Matt Clay or Beach Fix for some texture.” Think swept-back beach hair, just the commuter’s version.

The haircut: Short, sharp, and no messing about, the soft buzz is for the guys who are confident with clippers. “This is pretty close to a buzz cut, approximately a grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a very subtle taper around the edges,” says Maxwell Oakley, co-founder of luxury London barbershop Supply 91. Fair warning, though: there’s nowhere to hide. “It’s quite an exposing haircut, so it works particularly well on someone with a strong hairline,” he says. Square and oval faces with a strong jawline suit it best—but Oakley’s not one for hard rules: “If you wear something with enough confidence, you can pull anything off.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber to use clippers rather than scissors,” says Oakley. “A grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a subtle taper through the edges; you could also mention to lightly break into the fringe with the clippers to keep the front looking natural and textured.” Though it might seem simple, it’s so precise that you need someone who knows exactly what they’re doing—so always stick to a good barbershop.

How to style it: Less is more. “Avoid anything wet or greasy, as that can make the hair look sparse,” says Oakley. “Keep things matte to bring out the natural texture, with just a little bit of hold; work a small amount of product through for some definition without weighing it down.” Basically, a tiny bit of paste rubbed between your palms and through your hair, and you’re out the door before you can say “buzz cut.”

The haircut: Brent Pankhurst has been cutting hair for more than 30 years, from training under Vidal Sassoon to his shop in the Thom Sweeney townhouse in Mayfair, so he knows a good crop when he sees one. "It’s disheveled elegance,” he says. “It has a relaxed, slightly rugged feel, but the foundation is a strong, carefully considered shape.” It suits most faces, too, as long as the proportions are tailored to you. “For gentlemen approaching their forties and beyond, I often prefer a little more length through the sides for a softer, less harsh finish,” Pankhurst adds.

What to ask for: It all starts with the temples: “The most important thing with this haircut—and, for me, every man’s haircut—is to cut a square shape through the temple area,” says Pankhurst. Ask for scissors throughout, with scissor-over-comb on the back and sides, which “gives the haircut better structure and helps it hold its shape as it grows.” You don’t want to be going to the barbers every couple of weeks, after all. The top should be cut with texture for movement and separation; if your hair is thick, ask for more texturing to take out some bulk.

How to style it: Work some styling conditioner through damp hair, then give it a quick blow-dry using your fingers. Finish with a nickel-sized amount of molding cream. “Spread it evenly between your palms, then work it vigorously through the hair for that rugged, disheveled finish,” says Pankhurst.

The haircut: The slick-back, minus the Wall Street energy. “This is a textured slick-back or flow cut,” says Chris Merrick, head barber at Murdock Soho. “Keeping it relaxed rather than tightly slicked allows the natural movement and texture to come through.” Basically, it should look like you’ve just run your hands through it rather than set it hours earlier. It suits most faces, too. “It works particularly well on oval, square and rounder face shapes; styling the hair back opens up the face and highlights the cheekbones, while some height on top can help elongate a rounder face.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber for a textured slick-back or flow cut, with the sides slightly shorter than the top but enough length throughout to push the hair back,” says Merrick. “You want to retain movement and avoid a traditional short back and sides.”

How to style it: Towel dry your hair after showering, leaving it slightly damp, then work in a sea salt spray. “Blow-dry while using your fingers to guide the hair backwards and encourage natural movement,” says Merrick. Finish with a small amount of Murdock Matt Mud, worked between your hands and through the hair for a strong hold with a matte finish.

The haircut: Think of this as the haircut equivalent of rolling out of bed and looking like you meant to. “This is a classic textured cut with a messy, tousled finish,” says Guray Kesman, founder of luxury barbershop Kesman Grooming, “and it’s got that effortlessly undone look that Tom Hardy wears so well.” It’s also one of the most versatile cuts going: the fringe softens the forehead, so it suits oval, square, and longer faces, and it works best on straight to slightly wavy hair with some thickness.

What to ask for: “Ask for a scissor-cut textured crop, keeping the sides and back shorter but avoiding a harsh skin fade,” says Kesman. “You’ll want a good amount of length and movement through the top, with the fringe left slightly longer. The key is asking your barber to add texture rather than cutting everything too uniformly, so the hair has that relaxed, lived-in finish.”

How to style it: Work a small amount of lightweight styling cream or matte paste through slightly damp hair, then rough-dry it, directing the fringe forwards and slightly to one side. “Use your fingers to separate a few pieces,” says Kesman. “You don’t want it looking too polished or overly styled; a little movement is what makes this cut work.” When it looks like you’ve spent as little time as possible trying to perfect it, you’ve nailed it.

The haircut: If you were around in the ’90s, chances are you’re familiar with curtains—and you probably have the school photos to prove it. “To me, Tom Hardy’s curtains were something of an homage to Jonny Depp,” says Jay Hecker, master barber at Truefitt & Hill, the world’s oldest barbershop based in St. James’s, London. “We've seen this look before—it was everywhere when Brad Pitt broke though with Thelma & Louise and when David Beckham wore it in the ’90s—and you can trace it right back to old Hollywood’s Robert Mitchum.” Now, it’s making a comeback—and it suits most face shapes. “Because the hair is tucked behind the ears, face shape isn’t too much of a factor,” continues Hecker. “And if you want to accentuate your cheekbones, it’s definitely one to consider.”

What to ask for: “Ask for layers at the back to take out any weight, and keep most of the length on top so you can tuck it behind your ears,” says Hecker. A center part takes care of the rest. If you’re growing it out from a shorter cut, tell your barber so they can shape it in stages—just make sure you take a visual reference so you’re both on the same page.

How to style it: “Apply a cream like our C.A.R. cream or Circassian Cream to towel-dried hair,” says Hecker. “Then use a diffuser on your hairdryer on medium to low speed and heat for that textured finish.” The C.A.R. Cream has some history, too: Truefitt & Hill made it in the early 1900s for members of the Royal Automobile Club to keep their hair in check while driving open-top cars. Your day at the office, then, will probably be safe.

#6983. Alix Earle Quietly Supports Sofia Franklyn’s ‘Call Her Daddy’ Tell-All Book Amid Rumored Rift With Alex Cooper

Sofia Franklyn and Alex Cooper’s public falling out over Call Her Daddy may have gone down six years ago, but the drama seems to be alive and well, since the Sofia With an F host dropped a LOT more details in her new tell-all memoir, Daddy Issues.

She first announced Daddy Issues back in February, exclusively telling Rolling Stone, “The decision [to write the book] was one I came to on my own. Six years ago, I was reeling from an experience I didn’t understand. I needed to grow up and be free of ego as much as humanly possible.”

Alix Earle, who’s had her own beef with Alex ever since the Unwell Network dropped her Hot Mess podcast in February 2025, made sure to not-so-subtly support Sofia’s book on social media, posting a TikTok of herself reading Daddy Issues. Oh, and she also poked fun at that Interview magazine Q&A where she claimed she’s “never read a book,” adding text over her video that reads, “There’s a first time for everything!!!”

In case you aren’t aware of the layers this simple move has—and are still asking yourself, Wait, Alex Cooper and Alix Earle aren’t the same person?—allow us to catch you up to speed.

Sofia—who hosted Call Her Daddy with her bestie and college roommate Alex Cooper under Dave Portnoy’s company, Barstool—had a public falling-out in 2020 after they disagreed on negotiations with Barstool over the future of the show. While Sofia’s boyfriend at the time, Peter Nelson (aka “Suitman”), advised the girls not to renew their Barstool contract and take the show elsewhere, Alex wanted to sign on for one more year under the condition that she and Sofia owned the intellectual property of the pod.

In 2021, Alex continued Call Her Daddy without Sofia, eventually signing a $60 million deal with Spotify and another $125 million deal with SiriusXM in 2024. The latter includes exclusive advertising and distribution rights for Call Her Daddy, as well as the Unwell Network, her company.

Which brings us to Alex’s rumored rift with Alix Earle. When developing the Unwell Network, Alex signed the TikTok creator as one of the first podcasters under the company with Alix’s show, Hot Mess, in August 2023.

Things seemed to be going well until this time last year, when Variety reported that the Hot Mess podcast was dropped by the Unwell Network amid feud rumors between the social media personalities.

Sources gave a bit more context to the Alix vs. Alex situation with Us Weekly, saying, “They’ve had a lot of drama between them [since] they decided to collaborate on Unwell. They clashed over business, and their friendship is over because of it.”

While Alix has seemingly taken a hiatus from podcasting, Alex specified that “Alix not being able to podcast has nothing to do with Unwell” in a March 2025 TikTok post. She added that “Unwell gave her everything back” and that Alix owns her intellectual property.

As for Daddy Issues, Sofia also told Rolling Stone, “A book is reflective, vulnerable; it can’t be performative. The writing practice has been intense—sitting down, laying everything bare—but probably the most enriching thing I’ve ever done.”

#6978. The 25 Sexiest Movies on Prime Video Right Now

Including the best erotic thrillers, raunchy comedies, and all the mess in between.

Even in just the past decade, sexy cinema has come a long way from Fifty Shades of Grey. No judgement here—the entire trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video if you haven’t seen the Dakota Johnson and Jamie Dornan-starring franchise. But if you’re looking to expand your range a bit, we have a lot to recommend.

Sexy movies are having a comeback in 2026, thanks to Wuthering Heights, I Want Your Sex, and—another Olivia Wilde film—The Invite. While Emerald Fennell adapted Emily Bronte’s novel into a tortured and depraved sexual experience, Wilde’s I Want Your Sex and The Invite used sex more as a vehicle for dark comedy. But it’s not just new films on this list. We can also point you to Weekend, Ghost, Before Sunrise, and just any kind of sexy movie you’re looking for on screen.

Below, you’ll find our pick of the 25 sexiest movies you can stream on Prime Video right now.

Olivia Wilde and Cooper Hoffman star in Gregg Araki’s raunchy workplace romantic comedy about a high profile artist (Wilde) who ropes one of her new assistants (Hoffman) into a dominant/submissive relationship. At first, he enjoys the attention, but the rollercoaster of their sexual power dynamic eventually drives them both insane.

Sydney Sweeney does Rear Window meets Peeping Tom in this sexy crime drama about a couple who becomes obsessed with the sex lives of the people they can see from their apartment window across the street. The film also stars Justice Smith and Ahsoka’s Natasha Liu Bordizzo.

No Hard Feelings follows a down-on-her-luck Uber driver (Jennifer Lawrence) who is hired by a very wealthy couple to take their inexperienced nineteen-year-old son (Andrew Barth Feldman) on a date to boost his confidence before he leaves for college. The film treads dangerous waters, but Lawrence won over the audience and earned herself a Golden Globe nomination for the sexy comedy.

Love her or hate her, Emerald Fennell can make a steamy movie. Wuthering Heights is a little more of a reimagining than an adaptation, taking quite a few liberties with the text, but it puts the romantic and sexual dynamics right at the forefront of the story. And there’s no beating Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi as the leads.

The Fault in Our Stars director Josh Boone teams up with It Ends With Us novelist Colleen Hoover for a romance drama about a teen pregnancy that alters the course of two lovers’ lives. That is, until later in life, when the couple (Allison Wiliams and Dave Franco) reunite to explore what could have been.

Emerald Fennell’s steamy The Talented Mr. Ripley–esque thriller, Saltburn, follows a young student at Oxford University who becomes infatuated (read: dangerously obsessed) with one of his aristocratic classmates, who invites him to his family’s grand estate. It’s a darkly funny and wildly intense ride.

The phenom that saved dark romance novels: Fifty Shades of Grey and the entire Fifty Shades trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video. The story, for those who still don’t know, follows a recent college graduate (Dakota Johnson) who signs a contract to take part in a sexual bondage relationship with billionaire Christian Gray (Jamie Dornan).

L’inconnu du lac (known as Stranger by the Lake) is one of the most acclaimed erotic thrillers of the 21st century, picking up Best Director and the Queer Palm awards in the year it premiered at the Cannes Film Festival. In Stranger by the Lake, Franck, a man in his 30s, meets Michel by a lake where gay men often bathe and immediately falls for him. What he doesn’t know, however, is that Michel is a psychopathic murderer. Stranger by the Lake is as smart as it is sexy, and it will truly keep you on your toes.

Who said rom-coms can’t be sexy too? Secretary charts the dominant-submissive relationship that blossoms between a lawyer and his secretary. It’s certainly on the crazier side and may not be for everyone, but Secretary is a brilliantly acted film that feels quite far ahead of its time for such a mainstream movie in its original take on a romantic BDSM relationship.

Carol does something a little different: It’s a slow-burning romance with sensuality bubbling beneath the surface. It follows a department-store worker and aspiring photographer whose life takes an unexpected turn when she falls in love with a mysterious, beautiful older woman going through a difficult divorce. Todd Haynes’s Oscar-nominated period romance is a masterpiece of modern queer cinema, with career-defining performances from Rooney Mara and Cate Blanchett. Hugely romantic and remarkably executed, Carol is a special film that’ll certainly stick with you.

Alongside Four Weddings and a Funeral, Sirens was one of the films that brought generational British heartthrob Hugh Grant to American audiences. Grant plays a progressive reverend who, with his wife, pays a visit to an eccentric artist whose work is viewed as blasphemous. His wife soon finds herself drawn to the three models working with the painter, and what ensues is a clever breakdown of 1930s sexual repression.

Everyone loves a meet-cute, and Weekend’s is as brilliant as they come. Russell thinks he’s signed up for a one-night stand with Glen, a man he meets in a club, but over the course of a single weekend, a deeper relationship quickly grows between the two. Touching, oddly realistic, and distinctly British in its sense of humor, Weekend is sexy, funny, and grounded in a way that few romantic comedy-dramas manage.

In Eternity, Elizabeth Olsen plays a woman who, upon reaching the afterlife, is forced to choose between two people to spend eternity with: her first husband, who died young, and her second husband of 65 years. Eternity is infectiously sweet, a little sexy, and an essential modern comedy.

It might be a stretch to call this one “sexy,” per se, but you won’t find a romantic drama quite like this one—and you definitely won’t find one that’s more intense. Luca Guadagnino’s Bones and All is a road-trip drama that follows a young woman who develops feelings for a disillusioned young drifter on a 3,000-mile journey across America. Oh, and they’re both cannibals.

Wong Kar-wai’s romantic masterpiece proves that a film can be truly romantic, sensual, and intense while still holding solidly on to a PG rating. In the Mood for Love follows a man and a woman who discover that their spouses are having an affair with one another, but as they bond, they begin to develop their own connection. This is one for the yearners.

Sexy films are by no means a new phenomenon, and Caligula is one of the originals. Though the original was pretty poorly received, the film recently received a restoration and rerelease that reconstructed the film entirely from footage that didn’t make the final cut.

Love triangles don’t get much more complicated than this, and sports dramas don’t get much sexier. Challengers follows two friends who fall in love with the same young tennis prodigy and the complicated chaos that ensues when their relationships get messy. Tennis has never looked so thrilling

Set in the dreamy Italian countryside, Call Me by Your Name remains the film that introduced Timothée Chalamet to the masses as a future leading man (and got him his first Oscar nomination). Here he plays a teenager who falls in love with his father’s assistant and comes to terms with his sexuality. The film is sad, symbolic, and deeply romantic.

The legendary leading duo of Patrick Swayze and Demi Moore speaks for itself. Ghost is maybe a little more on the sentimental side than some of the other “sexy films” in Prime Video’s lineup, but it’s a deeply moving one worth your time.

Portrait of a Lady on Fire was an immediate hit with critics and audiences when it was released in 2019. The film follows an artist commissioned to paint an aristocratic heiress, but things become more complicated when the two fall in love. Romantic films don’t get more French than this one, which is also loosely based on the timeless tale of Orpheus and Eurydice.

Nicole Kidman and Harris Dickinson are an absolutely electrifying onscreen pair in this erotic thriller that sees a CEO begin an affair with her power-hungry intern, putting the stability of her family life and career on the line. Kidman picked up the Best Actress award at the Venice Film Festival for her role, and it’s been called one of her most ambitious performances to date.

Can anything capture your heart like a meet-cute romance? American traveler Jesse and French student Céline unceremoniously meet on a train from Budapest to Vienna, deciding on a whim to spend his last hours on the continent together. It’s the first in Richard Linklater’s acclaimed Before trilogy, and it sets you up for a brilliant slow burn.

If a Skarsgård signed up for it, I’m interested. He’s about to play a love interest made out of wicker, but last year Alexander Skarsgård played the mysteriously sexy biker-gang leader Ray in Pillion. The romantic black comedy tells the story of a shy man who begins a dominant-submissive relationship with the aforementioned biker, and it traces his personal growth as he navigates their dynamic. Pillion is sexy, crazy, and downright hilarious. It’s British black comedy at its finest.

Starring the king of crazy, surprisingly sexy indie movies, James Spader himself, Sex, Lies and Videotape is a true cult classic. The film revolves around a married couple with intimacy problems whose lives are changed when a mysterious drifter with a peculiar interest comes to stay with them.

David Cronenberg’s notorious erotic thriller is perhaps the most transgressive entry on this list, following a group of people who become sexually fixated on car crashes. Adapted from J.G. Ballard’s novel and starring James Spader, Holly Hunter, and Deborah Kara Unger, Crash is disturbing, provocative, and about as daring as erotic cinema gets.

#6976. I Asked Nail Artists in New York and LA—These Are the Most Popular Shades for Fall

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As an editor, it’s my job to report on beauty trends, whether that’s the buzzy skincare ingredient about to take over shelves or the haircut every celebrity will have in a few months. This year, I’ve reported countlessly on the biggest nail trends of 2026, from the minimalist designs all over Instagram to the nail colors on red carpets and runways. But what are real people actually asking for at the salon?

Now that we’re in the middle of fall, I asked a few nail artists all around the U.S. for the fall nail colors their clients are constantly requesting. “Fall’s nail palette is rich, nuanced, and far more versatile than the usual seasonal script,” says nail artist Elle Gerstein. Among the top trends, which I’ve outlined below, Gerstein says most people want shades that “look polished without feeling predictable and flatter a wide range of skin tones.”

Every single nail artist I spoke to called out deep browns as the most-requested nail color this fall. “Chocolate shades, from milk chocolate to rich espresso, are particularly trending,” says nail artist Vikki Swan. “These colors feel elegant and perfectly capture the fall aesthetic,” she says, noting that “brown tones are increasingly becoming an alternative to classic black, offering a softer, warmer look.”

Nail artist Viktoriia Voloshyna agrees, noting that “clients love it because it feels warm and cozy, but still very elegant, and it goes with all the fall neutrals people are wearing.”

Although dark nail colors are everywhere right now, don’t count out the classics. “Light nails are still having a major fall moment,” says Gerstein. “Sheer pinks and ashen lavender—especially in translucent, glassy finishes—feel fresh rather than summery,” she says. “Old rules of seasonal color are open to interpretation.”

For Voloshyna, “Milky and soft neutral shades are still very popular too. A lot of my clients have busy schedules and want a manicure that looks clean and polished for weeks.”

We apparently can’t get enough of all types of chocolate, including this burnished deep-red shade. “Chocolate cherry is particularly seductive because it combines the warmth of brown with that almost-vampiric red,” says Tucson-based nail artist Max Estrada.

“Browns and reds have a timeless appeal and can be a more interesting alternative to blacks and whites,” says Wong. She loves the MiniLuxe Pure Polish in shade Mexi-Cocoa for an in-between brown-and-red shade.

“Emerald is a fabulous wildcard; I love an unexpected jewel green when everyone else reaches for burgundy,” says Estrada. “But I want more than a flat coat of color. Give me dimensional velvet, reflective chrome, glassy shine, and glitter. Even a basic nude can become opulent with the right sparkling finish.”

“I’m seeing a continued love for effortless, barely-there manicures, and I expect that trend to carry into the holiday season, with subtle touches of sheer chrome and glitter for a festive feel,” says Wong. In fact, even Taylor Swift is trying it out, with a recent nude-and-glitter manicure. “Her look is a lovely reminder that the base color can be simple; add dazzling texture and extra length, and the look becomes something glamorous and memorable,” says Estrada.

“I’ve been seeing a lot of people ask for darker, moodier shades,” says Nicole Wong, nail artist and lead nail designer at MiniLuxe. “I think people naturally gravitate toward these colors as the weather gets cooler because there's this richness and warmth we associate with fall, while still feeling sophisticated and easy to wear.” If you want to opt for those deep shades but try something different, the experts say go for a cat-eye finish. “I expect to see more deep shades with interesting textures and finishes in the latter half of fall,” says Swan.

“Cat-eye nails continue to be one of our most requested styles, and we've noticed clients becoming more intentional about how they wear them,” says nail artist Sandra Wei. “Rather than going for an all-over, dramatic shimmer, many are gravitating toward softer, more diffused magnetic effects that subtly catch the light.”

“Clients are choosing cool blue shades this fall, both deep navy and soft light blue,” says Voloshyna. “Blue isn't a typical fall color, but the darker shades feel just as cozy and elegant as brown or burgundy, and light blue looks fresh and a little icy. It's a nice change for people who want something different from the classic fall palette.”

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan, where she covers skincare, makeup, hair, nails, and more across digital and print. She can generally be found in bright eyeshadow furiously typing her latest feature or hemming and hawing about a new product you "have to try." Prior to Cosmopolitan, she wrote and edited beauty content as an Editor at The Everygirl for four years. Follow her on Instagram for makeup selfies and a new hair 'do every few months.

#6974. The List of Possible Democratic Senate Committee Chairs Looks a Bit, Well, Old

Let’s worry about the septuagenarians and octogenarians later. For now, let’s grab some popcorn and watch them get to work.

Waiter? I’ll take everything on the menu.

Reporter Mona Burns has put together a list of possible committee chairmen once the new Democratic Senate convenes.

1) This list is a bit heavy on septuagenarians like, well, me.

2) The GOP already is hip deep in a leadership brawl that almost perfectly parallels the struggle over who was to blame for the bloodbath.

I’ll worry about my age cohort later. For now, I’ll put my feet up and enjoy the fact that Senator Professor Warren is already on the case. There is not enough popcorn in the world.

It’s Grammy Night on Dancing With the Stars, and there’s a twist! On top of performing to award-winning songs, the remaining couples (minus Harry Shum Jr. and Jenna Johnson) are competing in “dance-offs,” where they’ll be performing the same dance style to the same song—at the same time. A little confused as to how the whole thing goes down? Here’s what to expect tonight.

As the show puts it in their official press release: “Two at a time, couples face off against one another on the ballroom floor as they dance head-to-head in the same style at the same time. Winners of each of the three Dance-Offs will be selected by the judges, and the winning couple from each dance-off will receive bonus points added to their judges’ totals for the night.”

Here are the pairings, the songs, and the dance styles:

These two have immunity thanks to getting the highest scores last week! Which means judges will automatically add the dance-off bonus points to their score this week.

#6955. I've Had This Modular Sofa for Four Years, and Its Secret Twin "Bed" Is a Hosting Game-Changer

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The first couch I ordered for my basement was a bust. No matter how hard they tried, the delivery men couldn’t angle it to get it down the narrow stairs. Because I live in a 1930s home, all the charm turns to chaos when it comes to fitting new furniture. I was literally stuck because a family of five was visiting for the holidays and they had nothing to sit (or sleep) on. Plus, what even is a basement without a comfy couch?

Ideally, I wanted a sleeper sofa to accommodate my guests and let my kids have sleepovers. But there was absolutely no way I would risk buying another couch that couldn’t come apart and was stuck in a corner. For round two, I knew that I needed a modular sectional. After a bit of research, I came across the Burrow Range sectional and was elated that it could be assembled like a building-block puzzle that can grow over time if I wanted to add more sections.

Since the basement isn’t huge, my selection was a four-piece model that included two arm pieces as well as an ottoman. Because my dreams of a sleeper were no more, I liked that the Range has flippable top cushions that, when pulled back, could make a twin-size bed. Happily, four years later, the sectional has survived many, many sleepovers and now resides in my bedroom. Read on to see how it transitioned from child-hopping to a peaceful nook two floors up.

Best For: A growing household or someone who likes to change the layout of their space often.

Not ideal for: Someone who is very tall. If you are taller than 6 feet, the sofa may sit too low for you.

Price at review (4-piece sectional lounger): $1,271 (originally $2,119)

Bottom Line: The modular sectional is comfortable and won’t sag or end up with flat cushions, even after years of use.

Because I needed the sectional before the winter holidays, I was glad that I could order by early December and still get my sofa before Santa came to town. The four big boxes for the cushions and a little box for legs came to my front door within a week.

My husband and I brought down all the boxes to the basement where we started to unbox and assemble. The overall process took no more than 30 minutes. In a nutshell, you need to line up the holes with the metal braces and secure those with large plastic knob screws. The cushions have two strips of Velcro that stick to the black fabric at the base to keep them from sliding out from beneath.

The sofa pieces are easy to secure together.

Compared to my living room couch, which is a mid-century modern style West Elm reversible sectional, the Burrow has a bit more bounce to the cushion. While the seats are softer, I would still describe it as medium-firm. As soon as I sit down, it feels relaxing. Because it is only 28 inches high, it is a little lower than I am used to. (This still works out because I can slouch down and stretch my feet up on the ottoman when I watch a movie.)

Later, when I moved the sectional up to my bedroom, I lie across all three seat cushions, with my head resting on the arm cushion, as if I am reading in bed. The arm cushions are slightly angled, with more padding at the bottom to create a gentle incline that supports my head.

I chose the Stone Gray upholstery, an almost salt-and-pepper look, in performance flatweave. This is my third woven upholstery sofa, and it is the softest. It feels similar to a worn-in tweed jacket. I think it actually got a bit softer with age. I am happy to report there is absolutely no pilling or loose threads.

My biggest pet peeve is saggy couch cushions. I cannot stand it when the fabric looks crumpled on the seats, and there are sloppy valleys on the back cushions. After having this sectional for four years, I can vouch that it still looks the same as the day we assembled it. Which is a small miracle because when the Range lived in the basement, it was base camp for elementary-age boys playing video games and jumping around. If for some reason you wanted to fluff, you can unzip the underside of the back cushions and take out the filling to give it more shape.

In terms of stains and spills, if any kids spilled something on the sofa, I don’t know about it because it has no markings. Come to think of it, I don’t think I’ve ever had to clean the sofa beyond brushing off crumbs.

I did catch my cat trying to stretch (scratch) on the back of the sectional a few times, but I shooed him away, and he did not make it a habit. When I checked, there were never any loops or snags. (I cannot say the same about another woven sofa that is a victim of his claws.)

The cushions are still in terrific shape.

The flexibility of the Burrow Range sectional is everything. That’s the main reason I would highly recommend this couch. Not only do the pieces come in individual boxes that can fit through narrow doorways of older homes, but you can customize the sectional to your exact needs.

Originally, I needed something to help with hosting my out-of-town family, but this sectional has proven again and again that it’s a high-quality piece that can hold up for years to come. I still can’t believe that the cushions are in tip-top shape.

Pricewise, it’s a solid buy for mid-range furniture, especially because I don’t see a reason to replace it any time soon. Even when I ended up getting a new green modular sectional to go with the new wallpaper that I installed during my basement makeover, I found a spot for it in my bedroom. The Range now serves as a reading nook or a makeshift WFH station when I don’t want to stay in bed but feel too lazy to walk down the stairs to my home office. I added a Häti pouf with a scalloped EtúHome board next to the Burrow ottoman, and it serves as a perch for my coffee.

All in all, I am very happy with this purchase. It is durable, comfortable, and boasts clean lines and a silhouette that is approachable, but a bit more interesting than a standard traditional sofa.

#6954. Cole Hauser Is Going Undercover for the FBI. (Sort of.)

The Dutton Ranch actor will star in a new series about a real-life FBI agent who infiltrated neo-Nazi groups.

Cole Hauser went toe to toe with Texas as Dutton Ranch’s Rip Wheeler. Now, he’ll take on white supremacist groups, drug traffickers, and motorcycle gangs in a new series based on former undercover FBI agent Scott Payne’s 2025 memoir, Code Name: Pale Horse: How I Went Undercover to Expose America's Nazis.

According to Deadline, the Yellowstone actor is developing the project with members of Constantin Film (Resident Evil), with an aim to star in the lead role as well. The story would follow Payne, or a character much like him, who spent two decades working undercover in extremist groups to bring them to justice from the inside.

One scene from Payne’s memoir includes a Halloween night in which he had to slaughter and drink the blood of a goat with a violent neo-Nazi group in Georgia. He’s burned crosses with the Ku Klux Klan and smuggled drugs across the border—all to bring in hundreds of arrests for the FBI.

“Feeling so blessed! Let’s go!!!!” Payne reacted to the news on social media.

Dubbed “The Hillbilly Donnie Brasco,” Payne’s memoir also details the struggles of undercover work, as well as how he managed his marriage, his faith, and fatherhood throughout some of the most dangerous operations in FBI history.

“These are the type of stories that I am drawn to as a producer and as an actor,” Hauser told Deadline. “Scott Payne is a real-life hero, with the kind of moxie and edge you would need to risk your life to fight evil. I am looking forward to producing this movie and getting a chance to bring him and his story to life on the screen.”

Elsewhere, Hauser is also working on a series about the origins of modern-day poker with Sylvester Stallone titled Blood Aces: The Wild Ride of Benny Binion. He told Esquire in 2023 that he was in the early stages of a series where he and Matthew McConaughey would play brothers, written by True Detective’s Nick Pizzolatto. (Brothers may have gotten in the way of that one.) But Hauser’s also gearing up for the release of Dutton Ranch season 2, which will likely air sometime in 2027.

#6949. James Gunn’s DC Universe Was Weird, Ambitious, and Ultimately Unfinished

The writer-director is a casualty of Skydance’s acquisition of Warner Bros. He was building to a future that fans will now likely never see, for better or worse.

The hierarchy of power at DC Studios is changing again. On November 9, news broke that writer-director James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran left—later reported as ousted from—their jobs as co-CEOs and co-chairmen of DC Studios. Jeremy Latcham, a former Marvel Studios producer whose past credits include The Avengers and Gunn’s own Guardians of the Galaxy, will replace them on December 1.

Suddenly, DC fans woke up on a Monday to a new regime. But how will we look back on the four years that a prolific genre writer and director, who came up under Lloyd Kaufman’s B-movie studio Troma, oversaw an entire superhero universe?

It was a time of a lot of promise, not all of it fulfilled.

Before we start the eulogies for the DC Universe, it’s important to note that things aren’t over. Yet. Latcham will take over Gunn and Safran’s posts, so DC Studios is still up and running with more movies in the pipeline, many of which bear Gunn’s fingerprints. Man of Tomorrow, for example. The 2027 movie is a direct sequel to Superman, also written and directed by Gunn. Among its biggest moves is introducing a new Wonder Woman (rumored, but not confirmed, to be Adria Arjona). The Batman: Part II, the long-awaited sequel to Matt Reeves’s The Batman, will also open in 2028 with Gunn and Safran involved as producers. Gunn also said on Instagram they have been “engaged on another DC project which I’m writing.”

Both Gunn and Safran are expected to stay at DC, with Deadline confirming they will “continue to produce on various DC projects.” That said, Matthew Belloni at Puck published a story citing sources who say the DCU will reboot, again, after Man of Tomorrow. And at The Hollywood Reporter, Borys Kit and Aaron Crouch detailed the friction Gunn faced while at the studio, and how Gunn and Safran’s exits were forced by new Skydance leaders David Ellison and Ynon Kreiz. THR reports the DC heads felt secure after the big Warner-Paramount merger; Clayface had grossed $100 million, and Gunn was preparing a draft for the third Superman movie when they were told they were out. You can say the DCU is in a crisis right now.

For now, we can say that a critical chapter of DC is over. Gunn will no longer apply his vision and filter across all DC projects, which he must feel a little relieved about. By his own admission, which he wrote on Instagram on November 9, Gunn expressed feeling stretched thin, having to exert energy on movies and shows he wasn’t emotionally invested in to begin with. Said Gunn: “[T]he thing my ADHD hyperfocus has had the most difficulty with has been dividing my time between projects I’m incredibly passionate about and the ones I’m not so much and all the things I have to do as a studio head.”

Gunn and Safran got their jobs in late 2022, but it wasn’t until 2024 that people saw the first glimpse of their reign. Though Gunn had already worked for DC on 2021’s The Suicide Squad and created the spin-off Peacemaker, those projects were separate from other things at DC, then run by Walter Hamada. At the time, its biggest bet was Black Adam, Dwayne Johnson’s long-in-development antihero project that fell flat and led to nowhere. When Gunn took the reins of DC soon after, the former Marvel director had carte blanche to tailor the sandbox to his vision.

In January 2023, Gunn revealed his plans for DC Studios in something he called “Chapter 1: Gods and Monsters.” In a six-minute YouTube video, Gunn ran down his plans for the movies and shows that made up his master plan for the next decade of DC Studios. But for every mention of this movie or that HBO Max show, the first project carried significant weight. It was the first step, the real first impression, as a showcase of what Gunn would do with a DCU bent to his will. What icons would make it to the screen? What legend would get their spotlight? What iconic storylines might be retold on the big screen? What, really, will James Gunn’s DC become?

His first order of business was... Creature Commandos.

In fairness, Creature Commandos was a great show (if you saw it). It was an adult animated series where horror and pulp-inspired superheroes teamed up as a black ops strike force. While a show like that wouldn’t be anyone’s idea of a great introduction, Gunn’s background as the writer of movies like Tromeo & Juliet and his success with Guardians of the Galaxy for Marvel at least made sense. It basically said without ambiguity that this new DCU is being molded by no one else but Gunn.

Then, Superman premiered in summer 2025. Initially written by Gunn, the DC Studios head eventually named himself the director of DC’s first theatrical release under his stewardship. Despite my own misgivings about the movie, it was the reorientation a refreshed superhero universe needed. You simply can’t have a DC Universe without the Man of Steel, whose first appearance in Action Comics #1 in 1938 kicked off the modern superhero as we know it.

Superman made a solid first impression for DC at the cineplex. So there was plenty of optimism for the 2026 spin-off Supergirl starring Milly Alcock. Largely based on the acclaimed Tom King and Bilquis Evely graphic novel Supergirl: Woman of Tomorrow, the pseudo-sequel was poised to keep up DC’s newfound momentum. It didn’t. Supergirl hit a wall, much like Black Adam. No superhero universe is immune to duds, but given it was just DC’s second time at bat, a blemish on what still felt like a new, fresh-out-of-the-box DCU felt grave.

But one dud wasn’t enough to kneecap the DCU. The same summer saw the premiere of Lanterns, which fostered a faithful audience on HBO Sunday nights during its buzzy eight-episode first season. Despite mixed reviews, Clayface has grossed a profitable $100 million against a slim budget. Between the next Superman and Batman sequels, there was still plenty to look forward to. Until now.

James Gunn’s reign wasn’t impervious to bad news either. Since his January 2023 reveal, many of the projects have wound up on the back burner: The Authority, which had been a “passion project” for Gunn (with plenty of setup for it in Superman); Waller, a TV show with Viola Davis reprising her role from the Suicide Squad films; Paradise Lost, a political epic set in Wonder Woman’s home; and Booster Gold, a superhero comedy about a goofball from the future. And throughout his reign, Gunn put up with questions about his Batman (not Matt Reeves’s), which he would have introduced in The Brave and the Bold. Gunn sweated over the character so much that a cameo of Batman in Creature Commandos was shrouded in shadow, because he said he was still deciding on what the costume was going to look like.

Since the start of Gunn’s DC, it was clear his architecture came from a place of affection for the brand, and for very specific corners of it. His body of work reflected his scraggly upbringing as a punk creative, with empathy for characters who were too weird for their own Saturday morning cartoon. The Suicide Squad elevated such figures, like Weasel and Polka-Dot Man, whose climax involved a fight with Starro, a colossal evil starfish. Though his Superman was a sunny throwback to the Christopher Reeve classics, it was also jam-packed with the likes of Green Lantern, Mister Terrific, Metamorpho, and a villain Gunn was clearly setting up to be Bizarro.

Multiple times on social media, Gunn shared artwork from one DC book in particular: Kingdom Come, written by Mark Waid and illustrated by Alex Ross. Published in 1996, the story was a treatise of sorts by Waid, who lamented the state of superhero comics at the time. The story sees an older Superman who comes out of retirement to reform the Justice League, only to meet physical and philosophical resistance—predominantly from Batman.

Gunn’s allusions to Kingdom Come, including the exact shape of Superman’s “S” in his film, suggested he was laying the groundwork for a Kingdom Come-style event movie. It would have been an ambitious endeavor, oddly fitting to Gunn’s sensibilities. In the few years we saw Gunn at work for DC, he was building a universe where tried-and-true icons of superhero comics brushed up against the prickly and the bizarre. Where weirdness leaked from the orifices of a summer tentpole with an action figure set.

It often feels like Gunn was just getting started, but four years with two (soon three) movies written and directed by him, several TV shows—and Supergirl and Clayface, whatever you think of them—leave a big enough impression to suggest that Gunn’s DC had no shortage of imagination, even if his signature flourishes had grown familiar. Not every superhero film needs to have shock F-bombs and action scenes set to ironic licensed music, but Gunn had a lane. He excelled at staying in it.

It’s doubtful Gunn and Safran (who took on the business end of things) will stay on at DC under Skydance leadership for long. They will probably see Man of Tomorrow and The Batman: Part II through. If those movies do well, they might stick around to keep making movies, because for the most part, they have a magic that works. If the new movies fail to take off, then Gunn might finally find new stories to tell. It’s hard to say which path is the better one.

Gunn’s Kingdom Come, whatever it might have been, pointed to a future that never became a promise. In his farewell message, Gunn wrote that “the characters we set up will live on.” Whether the next era will let the DCU stay weird is another matter.

#6945. The 12 Best Amber Colognes to Buy Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are the season’s top cold-weather compliment getters.

It has been an unusually warm fall so far, but today, at least in New York City, there’s finally a chill in the air. I got so excited when I saw the temperature was in the low 50s this morning that I may have rushed the season a tad and put on a thick sweater under an insulated jacket. So, I was sweating in the subway, but at least I smelled good—because when it comes to cold-weather fragrance, I know what I’m doing.

If you’re looking to add to your winter cologne rotation, there’s no better place to start than a good amber scent. When you think of fragrance as an accessory, it becomes something you strive to match with your outfit, and amber colognes pair perfectly with corduroy, thick coats, and soft scarves. But what exactly does amber smell like? After all, the kind of amber that probably comes to mind—fossilized tree amber—doesn’t actually have much of a scent.

Well, when we say “amber” in the fragrance world, it really refers to a blend of natural resins and synthetics that combine to produce a warm, rich, and sweet smell. Some classic scent notes that make up the amber fragrance family include labdanum (a resinous extract from a Mediterranean shrub), benzoin (a sweet, balsamic tree resin), ambroxan (a synthetic fragrance molecule with a warm, woody aroma), vanilla, and tonka bean.

Here, we’ve pulled together our definitive list of the top amber scents for men, covering every vibe, lifestyle, and scent story you’re trying to tell. Whether you’re leaning into smoky sophistication, something on the sweeter side, or going for “I naturally smell this good,” there’s something here for everyone. And with the holidays fast approaching, consider these foolproof gift options for anyone on your list who appreciates smelling like they have their life together.

Inspired by the luxury fashion label’s most iconic men’s silhouette, this fragrance is designed to capture the essence of a tuxedo, using notes that convey sophistication and sharp black-tie tailoring into scent. Grounded in earthy patchouli and layered with subtle spice, it radiates a refined, confident energy that’s perfect for winter yet versatile enough for wearing it year-round.

The purpose of Maison Margiela’s Replica line is that the scents are made to capture memories and moods, the scent of a specific moment. By the Fireplace is easily one of the most evocative scents in the collection—my personal favorite, for what it’s worth—because smoke and warm chestnut instantly transport you somewhere near a warm crackling fire.

It starts with a bit of spice and smoke, and once it settles on the skin, that initial smokiness softens into a smooth, vanilla warmth. The rest of the fragrance leans very heavily into the sweetness of amber. It’s a compliment-getter, if that’s what you’re after.

If you want to channel your inner Alexander Skarsgård in Pillion (IYKYK) with a scent that radiates rugged confidence, subtle earthiness, and that intoxicating new-leather smell that only comes from a new pair of boots or a leather trench, look no further. Malin + Goetz’s Leather Eau de Parfum has been a staple in my collection for years

It’s leathery and musky, but it has a good hit of cedar that combines to leans it into the world of amber. It also has a lot of floral notes as it dries on the skin to balance it all out. It’s a nicely layered fragrance. A purist might not call this amber, but we think it’s well within the sweet, resinous world of these other fragrances.

Not ready to commit to the full bottle? The unisex cologne also comes as a perfume oil in a compact rollerball perfect for a trial run or as a stocking stuffer.

As I noted in my intro, amber fragrances can take on a variety of scent profiles, all capturing the warmth and depth of true amber. Rich gourmand options like Horace’s Vintage Vanilla are a great pick if you love scents inspired by the culinary world, but don’t worry, it’s expertly balanced with a mix of woodiness and spice so you won’t end up smelling like you went overboard on your marshmallow-y Victoria’s Secret body mist.

Don’t even want to smell like an edible vanilla confection? No problem. The brand’s signature Oud Rose is another solid amber-forward scent that leans on floral notes to balance its intensity.

Diptyque is another one of those bougie fragrance houses everyone recognizes, yet for some reason I rarely reach for their scents over my tried-and-true favorites. But after smelling L’Eau Trois Eau de Toilette, I might have to rethink that stance. This one immediately transported me to being in Italy and stepping into a Duomo, where incense and myrrh linger in the air.

It takes a surprisingly verdant and green approach to the amber family, but those resinous top notes anchor everything with a spicy-smoky warmth. The result is a scent that feels refined, atmospheric, and just mysterious enough to keep you coming back for another sniff.

Sure, amber scents can be sweet and gourmand, but they can also be spicy and sophisticated. Giardini di Toscana’s Bianco Oro is a great example of the latter. At first spritz, you mostly get spicy pink and black pepper with a backdrop of amber, but as the scent lingers, the rich amber notes take center stage along with smooth musks and creamy woods. As it fades, it becomes a bit powdery, but the trio of spices, amber, and musks are still there. It’s the perfect scent to wear out to a nice dinner or evening event, but it works just as well in the daytime.

It’s a general rule that amber and woods go great together, since amber provides a warm richness that blends well with dry, crisp woods. That combination is at the foundation of Dolce & Gabbana’s The One for Men EDP, but before we get there, we actually get a touch of bright and juicy citrus from Tarocco orange and grapefruit as well as a natural, herbal hint of clary sage at the heart that adds a bit of earthiness to this fragrance. Amber and woods mingle at the base, grounding the lighter, more vibrant opening in something warm and rich.

Guerlain makes a handful of luxury amber fragrances, but Tobacco Honey is the one that stands out from the rest. It’s an intense blend of—you guessed it—tobacco leaves and golden honey, which creates a beautifully bold contrast of smoky and sweet. It emphasizes the sweet side of the ambery tobacco accord at the heart of this scent, enveloping the dry tobacco notes in vanilla, tonka bean, and sesame to create a rich, creamy heart that gets lifted up by vibrant, sweet honey. If you’ve ever wanted to try a tobacco fragrance but worried it would smell too smoky (or too much like cigarettes), this is the one that will change your mind.

For something a bit lighter and more airy than the mostly rich and deep amber scents on this list, try Golden Morning from Lafco’s new (and first-ever) personal fragrance collection. Like a cup of hot chamomile tea with honey, it’s soothing, warm, and a little sweet. Bright and citrusy at the top with bergamot and apple blossom, it then introduces chamomile and a floral heart of lavender, violet leaf, and rose. The dry down is smooth and sweet with honey, tonka bean, amber, and musks.

Okay, I’m not saying the other fragrances on this list can’t also be considered “bougie,” but Maison Francis Kurkdjian Paris’ Baccarat Rouge 540 takes luxury to an entirely new level. And yes, while the price tag is particularly eye-watering, I have to admit: this scent is absolutely worth it. A perfectly balanced blend of woody, floral, and ambery notes, this fragrance uses high-quality ingredients with a higher molecular weight, meaning they cling to the skin longer and create a richer scent profile with impressive staying power. In short, a little goes a very long way with this one…if that helps you justify the price tag.

Sitting somewhere between gourmand and citrus on the fragrance spectrum, Arquiste’s L’Or de Louis is one of those amber scents that just radiates richness. The 24K gold flakes are a little too on-the-nose, but the the story behind the fragrance does a better job of telling a story of opulence. It was inspired by the moments when King Louis XIV and his court strolled Versailles’ Grand Orangery, enjoying the flowering orange trees warmed by bonfires.

It also won The Fragrance Foundation’s Indie Fragrance of the Year Award in 2024, so the hoity-toity storytelling is backed by a scent that actually smells…well…good. The result is a golden aura of perfectly blended smokiness, fruitiness, and depth. A fragrance truly fit for a king.

If your nose is on the more sensitive side and you’re looking for a warm, enveloping scent that doesn’t overwhelm with heavy muskiness, D’Orsay’s Te dire oui. V.H. Eau de Parfum is a perfect choice. Built on leather, it avoids the overpowering muskiness found in many amber and leather fragrances, opting instead for a cleaner, sportier interpretation rounded out by a subtle saffron and rose finish.

This aligns perfectly with the brand’s philosophy, which favors fragrances that act as personal rituals—subtle companions that are seamlessly woven into everyday life—rather than scents that dominate every room you enter.
